Since the ratio $\mathrm{Per}(E)/|E|^{1/2}$ of a bounded nonempty set of finite perimeter is at least $2\sqrt\pi$, with equality for disks, a family violating a uniform persistence bound must have, at every time $s$ of the window with $T-s\ge4\pi C\ell^2/c_0^2$, a high set $\{\omega(\cdot,s)>c_0D/2\}$ that is unbounded or has isoperimetric ratio $\mathrm{Per}(E)/|E|^{1/2}\ge c_0((T-s)/C)^{1/2}/\ell$ (such a set contains no disk of radius $4\ell/c_0$, by the circulation bound, so its inradius is small; a large ratio may reflect elongation, boundary complexity or many components). Lemma 7.40(c) is the counterpart of this estimate for the killed density of alternative (I). This criterion concerns the entire high set. One sufficient way to exclude a family with $T/\ell^2$ unbounded would be to find times $s$ with $(T-s)/\ell^2\to\infty$ at which the entire high set is bounded and its isoperimetric ratio $Q$ satisfies $Q^2=o((T-s)/\ell^2)$; no necessity of this route is claimed. The planar hypotheses do allow individual high components with arbitrarily large ratios throughout a window of about one core time. For $L\ge1$ put $k^2=1+L^{-2}$ and $\psi=-e^{-k^2t}\cos x\cos(y/L)$. The field $u=(-\partial_y\psi,\partial_x\psi)$ is an exact unforced solution with viscosity $1$, and $\omega=k^2e^{-k^2t}\cos x\cos(y/L)$ is a function of $\psi$. Its historical suprema are $M=1$, $D=\sqrt{1+L^{-4}}$ and $H=\sqrt{1+3L^{-4}}$ (Frobenius norms; with $\rho=L^{-1}$ the squared norms are bilinear in $\sin^2x$ and $\sin^2(y/L)$, so they are maximal at a corner, and $(1-\rho^2)^2,(1-\rho^2)^3\ge0$ decide the corners). Hence $MH\le2D^2$. For $0\le t\le\frac14$ one has $\omega(0,t)\ge e^{-1/2}\ge c_0D$ with $c_0=(2e)^{-1/2}$. At every such time $s$, consider the component of $\{\omega(s)>c_0D/2\}$ that contains the origin. It is convex, because the superlevel sets of $\cos x\cos(y/L)$ in $|x|<\frac\pi2$, $|y|<\frac{\pi L}2$ are convex ($\log\cos$ is concave). It contains the rectangle $|x|<\frac\pi4$, $|y|<\frac{\pi L}4$, since there $\cos x\cos(y/L)>\frac12$ while the threshold relative to the amplitude is at most $\frac12$. It lies in $|x|<\frac\pi2$, $|y|<\frac{\pi L}2$. So its area lies between $\pi^2L/4$ and $\pi^2L$, its perimeter is at least $\pi L$, and its isoperimetric ratio is at least $\sqrt L$, while $\frac14/\ell^2\in[\frac14,\frac12]$. The entire high set is unbounded by periodicity. These properties follow from the elementary computations indicated; the identities and the norm bounds were also checked symbolically and on a grid. The example shows that a large ratio of a single high component does not by itself imply long persistence. It does not refute a converse restricted to bounded entire high sets, and it excludes nothing about the planar persistence question. Without the $L^1$ assumption, without $H$ and without the shape condition, the planar question has a bound logarithmic in $\mathrm{Pe}$. Let $u$ be a smooth unforced solution on $\mathbb R^2\times[0,T]$, $T>0$ (viscosity $1$), with $|u|\le M$, $|\nabla u|\le D$ (Frobenius norm), $M,D>0$, and $\omega(0,T)\ge c_0D$ (so $c_0\le\sqrt2$, as $|\omega|\le\sqrt2|\nabla u|$); put $K=2D\ge\|\omega\|_\infty$, $a=c_0D$, $b=a/2$, $p=(a-b)/(K-b)=c_0/(4-c_0)$, $L=\log(2/p)$ and $R_\tau=M\tau+2\sqrt{\tau L}$. Then for every $0<\tau\le T$ $$\big|B_{R_\tau}(0)\cap\{\omega(\cdot,T-\tau)>c_0D/2\}\big|\ge\frac{\pi p^2}4\,\tau\,e^{c_0^2\tau/(16\ell^2)},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad\frac T{\ell^2}\le\frac{32}{c_0^2}\log\frac{256\,\mathrm{Pe}^2/c_0^2+64L}{p^2}.$$ Proof. (a) At each time and for $f\in H^1(\mathbb R^2)$, integration by parts gives $\int\omega f^2=-2\int f(u_2\partial_1f-u_1\partial_2f)$, so $|\int\omega f^2|\le2M\|f\|_2\|\nabla f\|_2$. For $0<b_0<b$, splitting the plane into $\{\omega>b_0\}$ and $\{\omega\le b_0\}$ and using $\omega\ge-K$ gives $b_0\|f\|_2^2\le2M\|f\|_2\|\nabla f\|_2+(K+b_0)\int_{\{\omega\le b_0\}}f^2$, and absorbing $2M\|f\|_2\|\nabla f\|_2\le\frac{b_0}2\|f\|_2^2+\frac{2M^2}{b_0}\|\nabla f\|_2^2$ gives $\|\nabla f\|_2^2+\frac{b_0(K+b_0)}{2M^2}\int_{\{\omega\le b_0\}}f^2\ge\frac{b_0^2}{4M^2}\|f\|_2^2$, whatever the geometry of $\{\omega>b_0\}$. (b) Let $dX_\rho=-u(X_\rho,T-\rho)\,d\rho+\sqrt2\,dB_\rho$, $X_0=0$, with $B$ a planar Brownian motion. By Itô's formula and the vorticity equation, $Y_\rho=\omega(X_\rho,T-\rho)$ is a bounded local martingale, hence a martingale. With $\sigma$ the first time with $Y_\sigma\le b$, optional stopping at $\sigma\wedge\tau$ gives $a\le b\,\mathbb P(\sigma\le\tau)+K\,\mathbb P(\sigma>\tau)$, so the event $E=\{\sigma>\tau\}$, on which $Y_\rho>b$ for all $\rho\le\tau$, has probability at least $p$. (c) Let $\chi$ be smooth with $0\le\chi\le1$, $\chi=1$ on $(-\infty,b_0]$, $\chi=0$ on $[b,\infty)$, and $n=b_0(K+b_0)/(2M^2)$. The sub-probability measure $g\mapsto\mathbb E[g(X_\tau)\exp(-n\int_0^\tau\chi(Y_\rho)\,d\rho)]$ (a diffusion killed by a smooth bounded potential, not a Dirichlet problem on the moving set $\{\omega>b\}$) has a density $q(\cdot,\tau)$, smooth for $\tau>0$, solving $\partial_\tau q=\Delta q+u(\cdot,T-\tau)\cdot\nabla q-n\chi(\omega(\cdot,T-\tau))q$ (here $\operatorname{div}u=0$ was used), $q(\cdot,0)=\delta_0$; so $q\ge0$, $\|q(\tau)\|_1\le1$ and $\frac d{d\tau}\|q\|_2^2=-2\|\nabla q\|_2^2-2n\int\chi(\omega)q^2$ for $\tau>0$ (comparison with the heat kernel by Girsanov's theorem gives $\|q(\tau)\|_2^2\le e^{M^2\tau/2}/(4\pi\tau)<\infty$, and the identity follows by testing against spatial cutoffs $\eta_R$, whose errors are $O((R^{-2}+MR^{-1})\|q\|_2^2)$; the differential inequalities below are integrated from $\varepsilon>0$ and $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, so nothing is asserted at the Dirac initial time). The Nash inequality $\|f\|_2^4\le\pi^{-1}\|f\|_1^2\|\nabla f\|_2^2$ (split Fourier space at radius $\rho$, bound $|\hat f|$ by $\|f\|_1/(2\pi)$ inside and use $\|\nabla f\|_2$ outside, then optimize $\rho$) gives $\|q(\tau/2)\|_2^2\le(\pi\tau)^{-1}$, and (a) together with $\int_{\{\omega\le b_0\}}q^2\le\int\chi(\omega)q^2$ shows that $\|q\|_2^2$ decays at rate at least $b_0^2/(2M^2)$, so $\|q(\tau)\|_2^2\le(\pi\tau)^{-1}e^{-b_0^2\tau/(4M^2)}$. (d) Since $|X_\tau|\le M\tau+\sqrt2|B_\tau|$ and $\mathbb P(|B_\tau|^2>s)=e^{-s/(2\tau)}$, $\mathbb P(|X_\tau|\ge R_\tau)\le p/2$. On $E$ the weight equals $1$ (as $\chi(Y_\rho)=0$) and $\omega(X_\tau,T-\tau)>b$, so with $A=B_{R_\tau}\cap\{\omega(\cdot,T-\tau)>b\}$ we get $\int_Aq(\tau)\ge\mathbb P(E\cap\{|X_\tau|<R_\tau\})\ge p/2$, and Cauchy-Schwarz gives $|A|\ge p^2/(4\|q(\tau)\|_2^2)\ge\frac{\pi p^2}4\tau e^{b_0^2\tau/(4M^2)}$; since $A$ does not depend on $b_0$, let $b_0\uparrow b$. For the bound on $T$, take $\tau=T$ and use $|A|\le\pi R_T^2\le\pi(2M^2T^2+8TL)$ and $M^2T=\mathrm{Pe}^2T/\ell^2$: $x=c_0^2T/(16\ell^2)$ satisfies $e^x\le\alpha x+\beta$ with $\alpha=128\mathrm{Pe}^2/(c_0^2p^2)$ and $\beta=32L/p^2\ge32\log2$, and since $x\le e^{x/2}$ for $x\ge0$, $e^x\le(\alpha+\beta)e^{x/2}$, so $x\le2\log(\alpha+\beta)\le2\log(2\alpha+2\beta)$. Running the same argument with $\|q\|_r$, $1<r\le2$, in place of $\|q\|_2$ (by (a) applied to $q^{r/2}$, $\int q^r$ decays at rate $(r-1)b_0^2/(rM^2)$ for the same $n$, since $4(r-1)\le r^2$; $\int q(d)^r\le\|q(d)\|_1^{2-r}\|q(d)\|_2^{2(r-1)}\le(2\pi d)^{1-r}$; and Hölder replaces Cauchy-Schwarz), with $b=\theta c_0D$, $0<\theta<1$, $p_\theta=(1-\theta)c_0/(2-\theta c_0)$, $L_\theta=\log(2/p_\theta)$, $R_{T,\theta}=MT+2\sqrt{TL_\theta}$, $A_\theta=B_{R_{T,\theta}}\cap\{\omega(\cdot,0)>\theta c_0D\}$ and $d=r\ell^2/(\theta^2c_0^2)$, gives $|A_\theta|\ge2\pi d\,(p_\theta/2)^{r/(r-1)}e^{\theta^2c_0^2(T-d)/(r\ell^2)}$ whenever $T\ge d$; since $T/\ell^2=O(\log\mathrm{Pe})$ by the explicit bound, comparison with $\pi R_{T,\theta}^2$ gives $\limsup T/(\ell^2\log\mathrm{Pe})\le2r/(\theta^2c_0^2)$ along any family with $\mathrm{Pe}\to\infty$ ($r,\theta$ fixed; members with $T<d$ contribute nothing, since then $T/\ell^2<r/(\theta^2c_0^2)$ is bounded), and only after this limit letting $r\downarrow1$, $\theta\uparrow1$ gives $\limsup T/(\ell^2\log\mathrm{Pe})\le2/c_0^2$ (an upper bound; its optimality is not claimed). The dependence on $\mathrm{Pe}$ is logarithmic, qualitatively like the logarithm in Theorem 7.18 for the three-dimensional ratio $\alpha_n$; the argument leaves open whether $T/\ell^2$ is bounded in terms of $c_0$ (and $C_*$) alone. The proof uses only that $\omega$ is a bounded solution of the drift-diffusion equation with the bounded divergence-free drift $u$ (incompressibility enters only the energy identity in (c)) and that, being the curl of a field bounded by $M$, it satisfies the circulation inequality (C) of Proposition 7.41 with $V=M$ (for $f\in H^1$, mollify $m=f^2/\|f\|_2^2$, for which $\|\nabla m\|_{L^1}\le2\|\nabla f\|_2/\|f\|_2$). Hence the same argument, with $V$ in place of $M$ in (a), applies to every bounded passive scalar $v$ obeying (C) at every time; with $a=v(0,T)$ and $K=\sup|v|$ its constants depend on $K/a$, and if $a\ge c_0D$, $a\ge K/2$ and $V\le C_VM$, then $T/\ell^2\le C(c_0,C_V)\log(2+\mathrm{Pe})$. After the rescaling $\tilde v_A=D_Av_A$, Proposition 7.42 gives such scalars with $K_A=D_A$, terminal value greater than $K_A/2$, $V_A\le(2\pi+o(1))M_A$ and $M_AH_A\le(4+o(1))D_A^2$, for which $T_A/\ell_A^2\ge c\log\mathrm{Pe}_A/\log\log\mathrm{Pe}_A$. So within this class of passive scalars the logarithmic upper bound is sharp up to a factor $\log\log\mathrm{Pe}$, and no argument that uses only bounded incompressible drift-diffusion, (C) with $V=O(M)$ and these amplitude and drift bounds can give a bound for $T/\ell^2$ independent of $\mathrm{Pe}$; a logarithm-free estimate for the vorticity must use further information, for instance the relation between $\omega$ and its own drift. Let $\sigma(t)=\sup_x\|S(x,t)\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ with $S=\frac12(\nabla u+\nabla u^{\mathsf T})$, and $H_a(b)=\int_a^b\exp(-2\int_a^r\sigma(q)\,dq)\,dr$ for $0\le a<b\le T$. For vorticity of either sign, $\|\omega(b)\|_\infty\le\|u(a)\|_\infty/\sqrt{H_a(b)}$; hence a logarithm-free bound follows from an additional uniform estimate $\int_{T/2}^T\sigma\le A$, namely $T\le2e^{2A}\ell^2/c_0^2$, and along every family with a common lower bound $c_0>0$ and $T/\ell^2\to\infty$ one has $\int_{T/2}^T\sigma\ge\frac12\log(c_0^2T/(2\ell^2))\to\infty$. The strain integral is always finite ($\sigma\le D/\sqrt2$, since $S$ is symmetric and trace-free), but the hypotheses provide no bound $A$ depending only on $c_0$ (example below). Proof: write $\omega(x,b)=\int\omega(\cdot,a)\,d\mu$, with $\mu$ the law at time $a$ of the backward diffusion started at $(x,b)$ as in (b), and $\omega(\cdot,a)=\operatorname{div}F$ with $F=(u_2,-u_1)(\cdot,a)$, $|F|\le\|u(a)\|_\infty$; for a density $f$ with finite Fisher information $I(f)=\int|\nabla f|^2/f$ one has $\int|\nabla f|\le I(f)^{1/2}$, so integration by parts against cutoffs and Cauchy-Schwarz give $|\int\operatorname{div}F\,f|\le I(f)^{1/2}\|u(a)\|_\infty$, and it suffices to show $I(\mu)\le1/H_a(b)$. Split the backward time interval into $N$ steps of length $\delta$, each an exact transport by the flow $\Phi$ of the backward drift followed by convolution with the heat kernel $G_\delta$ of time $\delta$. A transport over a time interval $J$ multiplies $I$ by at most $\exp(2\int_J\sigma)$ (with $\sigma$ evaluated at the corresponding physical times), because $\nabla(f\circ\Phi^{-1})=Q^{-\mathsf T}(\nabla f)\circ\Phi^{-1}$ with $Q=\nabla\Phi$, $\frac d{dr}|Q^{-\mathsf T}\xi|^2\le2\sigma|Q^{-\mathsf T}\xi|^2$ and $\det Q=1$. The convolution obeys Stam's inequality $1/I(f*G_\delta)\ge1/I(f)+\delta$: for independent $X\sim f$, $Y\sim G_\delta$ with scores $U$, $V$ ($\mathbb E|V|^2=1/\delta$), the score of $X+Y$ is the conditional expectation of $\theta U+(1-\theta)V$ given $X+Y$ for every $\theta\in[0,1]$, so $I(f*G_\delta)\le\theta^2I(f)+(1-\theta)^2/\delta$, and one takes $\theta=(1+\delta I(f))^{-1}$. Starting from a Gaussian of variance $2\varepsilon$ per coordinate ($I=1/\varepsilon$), the recurrence $1/I_{k+1}\ge e^{-2\int_{J_k}\sigma}/I_k+\delta$ gives $1/I\ge\varepsilon e^{-2\int_a^b\sigma}+(\text{a Riemann sum converging to }H_a(b))$ for the splitting laws. Couple the interpolated splitting scheme and the diffusion with the same initial value and Brownian path; their uniform distance is at most $\sqrt2D(b-a)e^{D(b-a)}$ times the modulus of continuity of the path at scale $\delta$, by Grönwall's inequality, and the passage from Gaussian initial data to the point start follows from the bound $e^{D(b-a)}|X_0^\varepsilon-x|$. Weak lower semicontinuity of Fisher information (it is the supremum of $\int(2\operatorname{div}\psi-|\psi|^2)\,d\nu$ over $\psi\in C^1_c$) passes the upper bounds on $I$ first to the diffusion with Gaussian initial data and then to the diffusion started at $x$, giving $I(\mu)\le H_a(b)^{-1}$. To see that no uniform strain-integral bound holds, fix $t_*>0$ and $0<c_0<1$, and let $M\to\infty$ in the heat-flow shear $u=(0,M\operatorname{erf}(x_1/(2\sqrt{t+t_*})))$, an exact solution with constant pressure, $\omega\ge0$, $D=M/\sqrt{\pi t_*}$ and $\ell=\sqrt{\pi t_*}$: it has $\omega(0,T)=c_0D$ at $T=t_*(c_0^{-2}-1)$, so $T/\ell^2$ is fixed, while $\int_{T/2}^T\sigma=\frac M{\sqrt\pi}(\sqrt{T+t_*}-\sqrt{T/2+t_*})\to\infty$. Like the logarithmic bound, this argument uses only incompressibility, the scalar equation $\partial_t\omega+u\cdot\nabla\omega=\Delta\omega$ and the identity $\omega=\operatorname{curl}u$; its necessary condition is weak, since the available bound $\int_{T/2}^T\sigma\le DT/(2\sqrt2)=\mathrm{Pe}\,T/(2\sqrt2\,\ell^2)$ does not supply a uniform bound. For nonnegative vorticity the sign gives the Morrey bound $0\le\int_{B_R(x)}\omega\le2\pi MR$ for all centres and radii, which yields $\|\omega(T)\|_\infty\le\frac{\sqrt\pi}2M/\sqrt T$ in the heat-flow case, including circular flows $u=V(|x|,t)e_\theta$ (there $u\cdot\nabla\omega=0$); the arguments here neither prove a logarithm-free bound for general nonnegative vorticity nor construct an active family with a fixed positive $c_0$ and $T/\ell^2\to\infty$. The estimate also says what a family violating a $\mathrm{Pe}$-independent bound must do: at every earlier time $T-\tau$ the set where $\omega>c_0D/2$ has normalized area $|A|/\ell^2\ge\frac{\pi p^2}4\frac\tau{\ell^2}e^{c_0^2\tau/(16\ell^2)}$ inside $B_{R_\tau}$, exponentially large in $\tau/\ell^2$, in particular at the initial time. If in addition the solution has finite energy $E=\|u(0)\|_2^2$, then $\|\nabla u\|_2=\|\omega\|_2$, the energy equality $E\ge2\int_0^T\|\omega\|_2^2\,dt$ and $\omega^2\ge c_0^2D^2/4$ on these sets give, with $k=c_0^2/(16\ell^2)$ and $\int_0^Ts\,e^{ks}ds\ge e^{kT}T/(2k)$ for $kT\ge2$, the bound $E\ge\pi p^2M^2T\,e^{c_0^2T/(16\ell^2)}$ whenever $c_0^2T\ge32\ell^2$; hence $T/\ell^2\le\max\{32/c_0^2,\,(16/c_0^2)\log(c_0^2E/(32\pi p^2M^2\ell^2))\}$. The logarithm is thus the logarithm of the scale-invariant number $E/(M\ell)^2$ of energy cells of size $\ell$, which the hypotheses do not bound. The argument is a quantitative form of the proof of Theorem 5.1 of Koch, Nadirashvili, Seregin and Sverak, which uses the strong maximum principle (their Lemma 2.1) to propagate near-maximal vorticity $\omega\ge\frac12\sup\omega$ to arbitrarily large parabolic cylinders $B(\bar x,R)\times(\bar t-R^2,\bar t)$ and contradicts $\int\!\int_{Q_R}\omega\,dx\,dt\ge\pi\sup\omega\,R^4/2$ with the circulation bound $\int\!\int_{Q_R}\omega=\int\!\int_{\partial B\times(\bar t-R^2,\bar t)}u\cdot\tau\le CR^3$; here near-maximality is replaced by the survival estimate (b), which needs only $\omega(0,T)\ge c_0D$, and the full disk by the spectral inequality (a), which needs no geometry of the high set. The closest quantitative results we found are not uniform in the Reynolds number: in the periodic strip $\mathbb R\times[0,L]$, Gallay and Slijepcevic (arXiv:1402.6563, Theorem 1.2) prove $(L^2\|\omega(t)\|_\infty/\nu)^2\le C(1+R_\omega)(R_u^2+R_\omega^2)L/\sqrt{\nu t}$ with $R_u=L\|u_0\|_\infty/\nu$, $R_\omega=L^2\|\omega_0\|_\infty/\nu$, so that the vorticity Reynolds number becomes small after a time at most $CR^6L^2/\nu$, $R=\max(R_u,R_\omega)$. Gallay's lecture notes (arXiv:1411.5156, Section 1, read here) state on the whole plane, for bounded velocity and bounded initial vorticity, $\|u(t)\|_\infty\le K\|u_0\|_\infty(1+\|\omega_0\|_\infty t)$ with a universal constant $K$ (Theorem 1.2, a revisited form of Zelik's estimate), say that a time-independent bound is not available in the general case, and record, for velocity and pressure periodic of period $L$ in one direction (viscosity $1$), $\|u(t)\|_\infty+t^{1/2}\|\omega(t)\|_\infty\le K\|u_0\|_\infty(1+R_u^5)$ with $R_u=L\|u_0\|_\infty$ (Theorem 1.3, from the work of Gallay and Slijepcevic). On the whole plane, Gallay and Rodrigues (Ann. Fac. Sci. Toulouse Math. 17 (2008), 719-733, Theorem 1.2) give a quantitative $L^1$ convergence estimate to a translated, time-shifted Oseen vortex for integrable vorticity satisfying a Gaussian localization condition; their Remark 4 reads the resulting approach-time upper bound as a bound on the lifetime of freely decaying turbulence, with double-exponential dependence $\exp(\exp(CR^2))$ on $R=\|\omega_0\|_{L^1}/\nu$ (absolute vorticity), and it also depends on the Gaussian moment, the localization time and the tolerance. Their entropy estimate for nonnegative integrable vorticity with finite second moment (Proposition 3.1) decays like $t^{-1/2}$ and gives a much smaller approach time under controlled moments; it is an $L^1$ statement and does not supply a pointwise vorticity bound in terms of a velocity bound. For passive scalars the gradient form of a drift-uniform decay estimate fails: Seregin, Silvestre, Sverak and Zlatos (arXiv:1010.6025, Theorem 1.3 and its proof) construct a bounded smooth divergence-free drift $b$ on $\mathbb R^2$ with bounded derivatives of every order and stream function $O(\ln|x|\ln\ln|x|)$, for which $-\Delta h+b\cdot\nabla h=0$ has a nonconstant bounded classical solution; taking $\theta(t)=h$ for all $t$ disproves $\|\nabla\theta(T)\|_\infty\le C\|\theta\|_\infty/\sqrt T$ for all $T>0$. This example does not by itself settle a circulation estimate for a scalar $\operatorname{curl}U$ with $U$ bounded, and the construction does not impose the Navier-Stokes coupling. (iv) By Corollary 7.24 it would suffice, in either alternative, to show that the full-space outward strain cannot accumulate: $\int_{t_n-T_n^*}^{t_n}\|(u^r/r)_+\|_{L^\infty}ds\le(\frac13-\epsilon)\log(1/\nu_n)$ for some $\epsilon>0$ and infinitely many $n$. The trivial bound is $D_nT_n^*=L_n\nu_n^{-5/3}$. Such a bound may be unattainable: if, for a set of times of measure comparable to $T_n^*$, there are points at radii comparable to $r_n$ where $u^r\ge cM_n$ (the points may change with time), then $K_n(T_n^*)\gtrsim M_nT_n^*/r_n\gtrsim\nu_n^{-2/3}$ by Corollary 7.17. Whether such an Eulerian configuration can occur is not established here; a single material ring cannot remain at radius $\asymp r_n$ while moving outward at speed $\asymp M_n$ for such a time. The formal scenario after Corollary 7.24, a pair of expanding opposite-sign cores, passes the scalar scaling tests of 8.1; its preparation, its companion flow and its stopped contributions remain unverified, so it is not a counterexample, and it is not ruled out by the scalar comparisons made here. (v) By Lemma 7.26(c) it would suffice to prove $S(t_n)=\sup_{[0,t_n]}\|u^\theta\|_\infty=O(1/\nu_n)=O(M_n^2/D_n)$ along a subsequence (which would contradict the proved divergence $S(t_n)\nu_n\to\infty$ of Lemmas 7.25(c) and 7.26(c)); more generally, with $\mathcal C_n$ the disk of Lemma 7.8 (on which $\omega^\theta(t_n)$ has the constant sign $\sigma_n$) and $\int_{\mathcal C_n}(\sigma_n\omega^\theta(t_n))_+dr\,dz\ge c_1/(2\pi\nu_n)$, any mechanism bounding this quantity by $o(1/\nu_n)$ would suffice (a retention bound against viscous cancellation of the dipolar source; it must account for the full surviving contribution, transported initial vorticity and all generated parts, not only for signed totals or for cancellation of the source alone). Neither is proved here. Lemma 7.26 (d) and Remark (3) there add two necessary features of any such configuration: accumulated inward strain $\gtrsim\frac13\log(1/\nu_n)$ over the history, and inflow on the swirl-bearing region. Lemma 7.26 (e) adds a conditional one (Remark (4) there): if the source budget is supplied by a single source dipole split by a plane, it needs a preparation time $\gg r_n/M_n$. Lemma 7.27 sharpens the first: $K_-(s_0,t_n)\ge(\frac14-o(1))\log(D_n/r_n)$, which is larger than $\frac13\log(1/\nu_n)$, and Lemma 7.36 improves this to $K_-(s_0,t_n)\ge\frac13\log(D_n/r_n)-C$; and Lemma 7.28 gives the localized form: the swirl pressure must act, critically weighted, on the backward adjoint density of the record core over the history (not necessarily at every time), and within the long window either the adjoint-weighted circulation is inherited from the beginning of the window, or the adjoint weight has unboundedly large vertical variation; Lemma 7.30 adds that over every window of length $O(r_n^2)$ a non-inherited record circulation is produced at radii $o(r_n)$ (the source pairing at radii $\ge\vartheta r_n$ is $o(1/\nu_n)$ there), a source analogue of the axis alternative of Theorem 7.12. (vi) A formal multi-cell scenario (formal, not verified, not a counterexample) shows that the sizes required by the budgets of Lemmas 7.22 and 7.25-7.28 can be matched by a formal configuration. With $N=1/\nu_n$ and $a=\ell_n$, so that $M_n=N/a$ and $D_n=N/a^2$, choose in addition $r_n=Na$ (equivalently $\alpha_n\nu_n=1$) and $aN^3\to0$, and place $N$ axial swirl cells $\Gamma=g\chi_1(r/a)\sum_j\zeta((z-z_j)/a)$ with a fixed $g\in(0,G]$, a fixed radial profile $\chi_1$ supported in $(1,2)$ (not the cutoff of Lemma 7.28) and disjoint axial supports. In each cell the source has $L^1$ rate $\asymp g^2/a^2$ of each sign, so in a time $\asymp a^2$ each cell produces circulation of order one of each sign and $\eta$ of size $a^{-3}=D_n/r_n$; together the cells supply the source budget $\asymp N=1/\nu_n$ of Lemma 7.26(b), with $Y$, $\mathcal B$ and $\int X\,dt$ of order $N$, $S\nu_n\asymp1/r_n\to\infty$ and $\int\|q\|_4^4dt\asymp g^4M_n^2/r_n$. If the like-signed parts are collected into one aggregate of size $R_0=N^{1/3}a$ and this aggregate then expands as a self-propelling meridional dipole (radius $\xi r_n$, core $a\xi^{-1/2}$, velocity $M_n\xi^{1/2}$, vorticity $D_n\xi$, $\xi$ from $N^{-2/3}$ to $1$, preserving the volume, the circulation and $\Omega/R$), the expansion takes a time $\asymp a^2$, accumulates outward strain $\int u^r/R\,dt=\frac23\log N$, and its final core energy and its dissipation are of order $N^3a=r_n/\nu_n^2\to0$; these sizes are compatible with the scalar restrictions of 8.1 that were checked. Lemma 7.28 adds a requirement: if the cell interval of length $O(a^2)$ supplies the circulation $\asymp N$ with $Q\lesssim g/a^2$, then $\sup V_\psi\gtrsim N/(Gg)$ on that interval, which a sign-selecting adjoint weight over $N$ cells would have to realize; no such adjoint solution has been constructed. Unverified: the preparation of the cells from one fixed datum, the separation of signs and the merging of like signs without loss of circulation, the Biot-Savart velocity, the pressure and the energy transfer that the expanding core needs (the assigned core energy $U^2Rd^2$ grows by a factor $N^{2/3}$ during the expansion, so the surrounding flow must supply it; a comparable statement for the total Biot-Savart energy requires the dipole geometry, since opposite cores at separation $L\gg d$ contribute an extra factor $\log(L/d)$), and the stopped contributions of Theorem 7.12. Excluding Case 3 therefore needs a mechanism beyond these scalar budgets, for instance control of sign-selective transport (the source-adjoint correlation of Lemma 7.28) or of the energy supply of an expanding core.


## 5. How the verification was done

* Self-check: the verification described here consists of manual review, recomputation of the displayed constants and exponents, symbolic checks of the stated identities, and additional review of the passages discussed below; no formal certificate is provided. Every proof was written out with explicit constants and re-derived after each audit. The cylindrical equations for $\Gamma$, $q$, $\eta$ and the Cartesian identity $\partial^2_{x_2}u_2=\partial_rf/r-f/r^2$ at $(r,0,z)$ were verified with a computer algebra system (zero residuals).
* Independent audit: a second model (GPT-6) received the problem statement and the manuscript in repeated adversarial passes, each asked to break specific lemmas and to give a verdict per statement, and in research passes that reconstructed the main estimates from the problem statement alone (reaching the same first missing implication) and proposed further lemmas, each of which was re-derived here, with its constants, before inclusion. The corrections prompted by these passes were of the following kinds: missing hypotheses and degenerate cases, quantifiers and orders of limits, cutoff and boundary-term justifications (in particular integrations along the whole axis), the stopped events in the stochastic representations, constants, one false intermediate inequality and one claimed divergence that is false without an extra condition, and overstated scope in remarks and in the discussion of the gap; all were repaired in the text, and the passes found no fatal error in the final proofs. A block-by-block re-audit of the results numbered 7.10 to 7.40 and their remarks found no wrong estimate or constant (the later additions, Lemma 7.40(c), Propositions 7.41 to 7.43, Lemma 7.44, Corollary 7.45, Lemma 7.46 and the remarks of Proposition 7.42, were audited individually, by hand and by adversarial passes, as they were entered); its repairs were of the same kinds: the domain of two integrals of the adjoint weight, the solution class of an auxiliary linear problem, the quantified class of a counterexample, the time range of a preparation-time remark, the degenerate zero-datum cases, and the description of Lemma 7.38 as a refinement of the logarithm of Theorem 7.18 rather than an identification. The final proofs should be assessed from their arguments in Section 4. Supplementary numerical calculations reported in Sections 3 and 6 are not proofs; the analytic claims of Section 6 are identified individually as proved there, stated without proof, or conditional model calculations; they are not used in Section 4. The audits and the research passes used the same second model, so they are not independent of one another. Model-assisted review was part of the verification process described in this section; the mathematical content and limitations of each result are stated with that result.
* Literature: the Liouville theorems used (Koch, Nadirashvili, Seregin, Sverak, Acta Math. 203 (2009), Theorems 5.1, 5.2, Remark 6.1, Lemma 6.1) were checked against the paper. No published result excluding Case 3 under the present hypotheses was identified in the inspected sources; this was not a complete literature search. A separate literature consultation (second model, reading the papers) checked the hypotheses of Chen-Strain-Tsai-Yau (arXiv 0709.4230, Theorem 1.1: $|u|\le C r^{-1+\varepsilon}|t|^{-\varepsilon/2}$ with $0\le\varepsilon\le1$, for axisymmetric strong solutions on $\mathbb R^3$ with $H^{1/2}$ data, bounded $ru^\theta_0$ and pressure in $L^{5/3}_{t,x}$), Koch-Nadirashvili-Seregin-Sverak (0709.3599), Lei-Zhang (1008.4172, 1011.5066, 1505.02628: $|\Gamma|\le C|\log r|^{-2}$), Wei (1508.03318: $|\Gamma|\le|\log r|^{-3/2}$) and Seregin (2006.04140, 2201.00153); each needs a uniform space-time bound (Type I, $|u|\lesssim1/r$ up to logarithms, a BMO potential, or a quantitative decay of $\Gamma$ at the axis) that is not available in Case 3. On the numerical side, the computation of Hou (2107.06509, Section 3.4.1), with spatially constant viscosity changed once during the run, reports $\|u\|_\infty\sim s^{-1/2}$, $\|\omega\|_\infty\sim s^{-1}$ (up to a logarithm) and a single spatial scale $\sim s^{1/2}$ for both the distance to the axis and the core, so that, at the level of leading powers and if these fitted scales are identified with the record radius and the core length (an identification not established, and logarithms not controlled), $\alpha$ and $\nu$ are of order one, the boundary of Case 3 rather than Case 3; the fits are not uniform bounds up to the singular time. The thin-ring computation of Hou-Huang (2102.06663) uses a degenerate viscosity, and with its fitted rates $\|u\|_\infty\sim s^{-1/2}$, $\|\omega\|_\infty\sim s^{-3/2}$ a fixed positive viscosity would give $\nu\to\infty$. No inspected standard Navier-Stokes scenario has $\alpha\to\infty$ and $\nu\to0$; an exclusion of Case 3 would therefore not contradict the inspected numerics, and the numerics do not show that Case 3 is unrealizable. For the planar persistence question we read the abstracts of Gallay and Sverak (arXiv 2301.01092) and Donati and Gallay (arXiv 2503.20643) and the arXiv listing entry of Gallay (arXiv 0908.2518); this is not a verification of their full theorem statements and not a literature survey. The first abstract describes an axisymmetric swirl-free ring issued from a circular filament of circulation $\Gamma>0$ and initial radius $r_0$, which stays close to the translated heat-flow profile of core scale $\sqrt{\nu_{\rm visc}t}$ ($\nu_{\rm visc}$ the viscosity) on a time interval of scale $(r_0^2/\Gamma)(\Gamma/\nu_{\rm visc})^\sigma$ with an exponent $\sigma<1/3$; the precise quantifier on $\sigma$ and the other hypotheses were not checked. The second concerns a concentrated vortex in an external flow (with enhanced dissipation) and the third, from its listing entry only, interacting point vortices. The statements described in the inspected abstracts concern specialized classes and do not supply the persistence estimate required here; the complete theorem statements and other results in those papers were not checked. We also read the abstracts (only) of several recent axisymmetric preprints (arXiv 2604.21213, 2604.03519, 2605.09797, 2604.09949, 2605.18011, 2507.14964, 2605.01875, 2606.07869) and of Jiu and Xin (arXiv 1501.02412); at the abstract level each is conditional (a scale-invariant or critical smallness hypothesis, a swirl integrability criterion, a weighted coercivity computation, explicit structural hypotheses, or a model problem) and none was found to exclude Case 3. The abstract of arXiv 2606.07869 states that meridional Type-II records, signed critical compression, noncompact high-frequency behavior and boundary-entry alternatives remain open there. None is used. For the Reynolds-uniform planar decay question of Section 1.3 a second literature consultation by the second model found neither a proof nor a counterexample. Of the items it reported, Sections 1-3 of Gallay and Rodrigues (Ann. Fac. Sci. Toulouse Math. 17 (2008)) and Theorem 1.3 of Seregin, Silvestre, Sverak and Zlatos (arXiv 1010.6025) were read here; the others (Beekie, Chen and Jia, arXiv 2403.13104, on linearized perturbations of periodic shear flows; Dolce and Gallay, arXiv 2407.13562, on a viscous vortex dipole) carry additional hypotheses according to that consultation and were not checked here. A third consultation by the second model (2024-2026 sources, a scoped search) again found no result supplying either missing step. For five of the items it reported we read the abstracts here. Q. S. Zhang (arXiv 2604.07785) proves regularity for axisymmetric Leray-Hopf solutions under the one-sided bound $u^r\ge-C(T-t)^{-1/2}$ and bounded initial circulation; Case 3 gives no such bound on inward radial velocity. Lei, Ren and Tian (arXiv 2501.08976) prove regularity when the vorticity directions in the high-vorticity region lie in a double cone; a ring with dominant azimuthal vorticity has directions $\pm e_\theta$ along a full great circle, so the hypothesis fails. Choi and Jeong (arXiv 2110.09079) prove monotone growth of the vorticity impulse for swirl-free Euler flows that are odd in $z$ with one sign in each half space; Case 3 has neither the symmetry nor the sign. Lim and Jeong (arXiv 2409.19497) bound the vorticity growth of swirl-free Euler flows with compactly supported vorticity by $t^{4/3}$ through a velocity bound from energy and conserved quantities; with viscosity and swirl those quantities are not conserved. Guo, Jeong and Zhao (arXiv 2602.20131) prove global concentration of a single thin swirl-free Euler ring and, by their abstract, linear-in-time stretching of its support; concentration of the vorticity mass therefore does not imply that the support stays short in $z$, and their hypotheses (one sign, the invariants of one thin ring) are not available for the record core. The same consultation reports that Corollary 6.3 of arXiv 2605.01875v4 absorbs the outward strain only under an assumed weighted form bound, with the swirl source left to be estimated; this was not checked here. None of these results is used. The abstract of Duraiswami (arXiv 2609.17642) refers to a finite-time blow-up construction for the forced Navier-Stokes equations released by OpenAI in September 2026, whose object is an axisymmetric swirl core in anisotropic similarity variables with an axial through-flow; we did not inspect that construction. It concerns forced equations and so does not settle the unforced fixed-datum problem, and the abstract does not say whether its gradient records satisfy $r_nD_n/M_n\to\infty$ (an on-axis core would not by itself decide this, since Case 3 also has $r_n\to0$, Theorem 1, item 4). We draw no conclusion about Case 3 from it.
* Symbolic and numerical checks verify algebraic identities and explicit test cases only; they do not certify the analytic or stochastic arguments, which were checked by hand and by the audits.
* The reported model audits are fallible checks, not formal certificates, and neither missing exclusion has been proved.

## 6. Limitations and open questions

1. **Case 3 remains unresolved in this report.** No argument here proves TARGET-3, $\lim_{A\to\infty}\limsup_nD(t_n-A/D_n)/D_n=0$ (limits in this order), which by Theorem 2 is equivalent to the full exclusion. Because $A$ is fixed while $n\to\infty$, the relevant normalized times satisfy $A\ll\alpha_n$, where Lemma 7.1 gives persistence for all sufficiently large $n$; a decay estimate proving TARGET-3 would therefore contradict that persistence and exclude Case 3. Decay of $D(t_n-A_n/D_n)/D_n$ along normalized times $A_n\gg\alpha_n$ would not suffice, and a localized version must control the vorticity on the backward-characteristic ball of radius $(R_0+A)\ell_n$.
2. **Where the gap sits.** The persistence window $\delta r_n/M_n$ is the meridional transport time over a fixed fraction of the distance to the axis, and it is sharp for the comparison method; by item 15 of Theorem 1 it lasts at most logarithmically many viscous core times. The annular swirl source is controlled on the stated windows (by the circulation bound it stays small on $\{r\ge\vartheta r_n\}$ for times $\ll\vartheta^3r_n^2$); the remaining obstructions are the long annular persistence of alternative (I) and the net inner-boundary contribution, from radii $o(r_n)$, of alternative (II). Beyond the window one would need both of the following, since along a given subsequence either alternative may occur and excluding only one of them does not exclude Case 3: a depletion estimate for the net inner-boundary contribution in alternative (II) of Theorem 1, item 11, and an argument excluding the persistent killed homogeneous contribution of alternative (I) in the remaining logarithmic regime $\alpha_n^2=O(\log(r_nM_n))$ (item 16), for instance a version of item 14 without the logarithm, which by item 18 must use the Navier-Stokes dynamics and not only finite energy and the instantaneous Biot-Savart law. Each of the sufficient conditions listed in Section 1.3 (a bound on the vertical spread, tightness of the high part of the density, a gradient-weighted occupation bound, and the logarithm-free isoperimetric criterion of item 32) would exclude (I) at a fixed scale; none is proved here. A second sufficient route for (I) goes through the source form of item 16: (I) at the scale $\vartheta$ would be excluded by an upper bound $\int_{s_0}^{t_n-T_\vartheta/2}\|\partial_z(q^2)\|_{L^1}dt\le r_n^2D_ne^{o(\alpha_n^2)}$, or by an inward strain clock bound $K_-(s_0,t_n)\le\frac13\log(r_n^2D_n)+o(\alpha_n^2)$; the available upper bound for the source integral, $o(S)$ with $S\le CD_n^{2/3}M_n^{-1/3}$, does not give either. The velocity bound of item 14 controls the killed part of the representation of $\eta$ but not the inner-boundary contribution. Sign structure alone does not supply the depletion for alternative (II). The kernel of Lemma 7.14 is odd in $z-y_3$, but outward strain does not require an opposite-sign value of $\eta$: for example, a nonnegative thin toroidal distribution consisting of a bump symmetric about the observation height and additional mass below it has $u^r/r>0$ at an interior point where $\eta>0$ (mass below the observation height contributes positively, and the sign is decided by kernel-weighted, not unweighted, masses). If $V$ and $W>0$ solve the same homogeneous equation $\partial_tV+b\cdot\nabla V=\Delta_5V$, the product rule gives $(\partial_t+b\cdot\nabla-\Delta_5)(V^2/W)=-2W|\nabla(V/W)|^2$. Applied to the boundary-driven solutions with inner data the difference and the sum of the positive and negative parts of $v_n$ on $\{r=\vartheta_nr_n\}$ (zero outer and initial data), this gives, for each horizon $h$, $|I_n(\vartheta_n,h)|=W_h(1-\mathcal D_h)^{1/2}$, where $W_h$ is the unsigned inner contribution and $\mathcal D_h\in[0,1]$ is twice the expected occupation integral of $|\nabla(V/W)|^2$ along the stopped backward process under the law reweighted by the normalized absolute boundary value. For horizons with $W_h>0$, $\mathcal D_h$ is defined by this normalized absolute-boundary law; when $W_h=0$, both the signed and the unsigned contributions vanish and we set $\mathcal D_h=0$. Since $|v_n|\le\sqrt2\min\{H_n,D_n/(\vartheta_nr_n)\}$ there, $J_n/a_n\le C_0\Xi_n\sup_hp_h(1-\mathcal D_h)^{1/2}$ with $p_h$ the inner hitting probability, $\Xi_n=\min\{\vartheta_n^{-1},C_*\alpha_n\}$ and $C_0$ depending only on $\kappa_0$; so (II) would be excluded by $\mathcal D_h\ge1-(8C_0\Xi_n)^{-2}$ on the horizons $h\le T_{\vartheta_n}$ with $C_0\Xi_np_h>1/8$. A prescribed-drift scalar model shows that expansion, incompressibility and scalar diffusion do not force such a bound: take the linear axisymmetric strain $(u^r,u^z)=\sigma(r,-2z)$, $\sigma=M_n/(Lr_n)$ with $L$ fixed, and a two-signed scalar depending only on $z$ and $t$ (for which the radial terms of the operator vanish), with the same terminal parameters $M_n,r_n,\ell_n$ as the formal expanding pair after Corollary 7.24. The time integral of $d^{-2}$, with $d$ the distance between the two transported lobes, decreasing to $\ell_n$, is of the same order $\alpha_n\nu_n$ as for that pair and tends to $0$ in the logarithmic family of Section 4.8, so cross-diffusion is negligible; and within the horizon $\log(2/\vartheta)/\sigma$, slightly longer than the deterministic arrival time and shorter than $T_\vartheta$ when $\vartheta^3r_nM_n\gg\log(1/\vartheta)$, the backward process reaches the inner cylinder with probability $1-O(\log(2/\vartheta)/(\vartheta^2r_nM_n))$ (the radius is that of a four-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process). The model's velocity is irrotational and unbounded and its scalar is not its vorticity, so it is not a Navier-Stokes solution and excludes nothing; the identity and the model computations were checked here and in an adversarial review. Such a depletion estimate would require additional structure of the actual Navier-Stokes solution, for example the coupling between $\eta$ and the velocity. The coupling between $\eta$ and the velocity at a single time does not supply it on a prescribed range of $\vartheta$. The field $u^r=arz$, $u^z=-az^2$, $u^\theta=0$ ($a>0$) is a stationary solution of the unforced Navier-Stokes equations with pressure $-\frac12a^2z^4-2az$ (unbounded velocity, infinite energy), with $\omega^\theta=ar$ and $\eta\equiv a$. Its trajectories keep $rz$ constant and have constant radial speed $a\,rz$, outward for $z>0$; so $\eta$ is exactly conserved, viscosity included, while on a trajectory with $z>0$ the vorticity $\omega^\theta$ grows by the factor $1/\vartheta$ between $r=\vartheta\rho$ and $r=\rho$. For the backward process of the $\eta$ equation of this frozen field, $dR=(3/R-aRZ)\,dh+\sqrt2\,dB_r$, $dZ=aZ^2\,dh+\sqrt2\,dB_z$, considered up to its exit from a bounded region away from the axis, the product $RZ$ has drift $3Z/R$ only. Put $Y=\lambda\rho$, $U=aY$, $X=\rho U$ and $\varepsilon=\rho/(U\lambda^2)$, and start at $(\rho,\lambda)$. With the model scales $M_0=U$ and $D_0=a\rho$, which are comparable to the velocity and its gradient there when $\lambda\ll\rho$, one has $\rho D_0/M_0=\rho/\lambda$ and $\varepsilon=(\rho D_0/M_0)(D_0/M_0^2)$, so $\lambda$, $\rho/\lambda$ and $\varepsilon$ play the roles of $\ell$, $\alpha$ and $\alpha\nu$. Let $\vartheta\le\frac14$, $\vartheta^2\rho/\lambda\ge64$ and $\vartheta^2X\ge120$, and stop the process when $R$ leaves $(\vartheta\rho,2\rho)$, when $RZ$ leaves $(\frac34Y,\frac54Y)$, or at the age $h=4\rho/U$. The maximal inequality $\mathbb P(\sup_{t\le h}|N_t|\ge b)\le\mathbb E[N_h^2]/b^2$, applied to the martingale part of $RZ$ (quadratic variation at most $10\rho^2h$) with $b=Y/8$ and to $\sqrt2B_r$ with $b=\rho/2$, together with the bound $15Y/(\vartheta^2X)\le Y/8$ for the drift part of $RZ$ and the bound $3/(\vartheta\rho)-\frac34U\le-\frac12U$ for the radial drift, shows that with probability at least $1-2560\varepsilon-32/X$ the process reaches $\{r=\vartheta\rho\}$ before the age $h$ and before $\{r=2\rho\}$, with $RZ\in(\frac78Y,\frac54Y)$ up to that time. A compactly supported version is obtained by writing the stream function as $\psi=-\frac12ar^2z^2=-\frac12aY^2s^2$ with $s=rz/Y$ (so that $u^r=-\partial_z\psi/r$, $u^z=\partial_r\psi/r$) and replacing it by $-\frac12aY^2f(r)s^2\chi(s)$, where $f=1$ on $[\vartheta_0\rho,2\rho]$, $f$ is supported in $(\frac12\vartheta_0\rho,3\rho)$ with $|r^jf^{(j)}|\le C$ for $j\le3$, and $\chi=1$ on $[\frac34,\frac54]$ is supported in $(\frac12,\frac32)$. If $\vartheta_0\le\frac14$ and $\vartheta_0^2\rho/\lambda\ge64$, then $Y/r^2\le\frac1{16}$ on the support; the field is smooth, compactly supported and divergence free, and agrees with the stationary one on the stopping region for every $\vartheta\in[\vartheta_0,\frac14]$; the explicit formula for $\eta$ of the cut-off stream function gives $|\eta|\le C_\eta a$ everywhere; and counting derivatives ($\partial_r$ costs $1/r$, $\partial_z$ costs $r/Y\ge16/r$) gives $M\le CaY$, $D\le Ca\rho$ and $\|\nabla^2u\|_\infty\le Ca\rho/\lambda$, hence $MH\le Ca^2\rho^2\le CD^2$ since $D\ge a\rho$ at the starting point, with constants depending only on the profiles of $f$ and $\chi$. Its frozen-drift signed inner contribution at the age $4\rho/U$ is therefore at least $a[1-(1+C_\eta)(2560\varepsilon+32/X)]$ for every $\vartheta\in[\vartheta_0,\frac14]$ with $\vartheta_0^2X\ge120$, and at least $a/2$ once $\varepsilon$ is below and $X$ above constants depending only on the profiles. The stationary solution, the formula for $\eta$ of the cut-off stream function, the probability estimate with its constants and the derivative count were checked here and in an independent review. The example has limits. The cut-off field is not a stationary Navier-Stokes solution (its $\eta$ fails the stationary $\eta$ equation in the transition layers), and a frozen snapshot carries no information on the time-dependent production and ejection. Its inner contribution vanishes for $\vartheta<\vartheta_0/2$, where $\eta=0$, so it does not exclude a single-time bound at a smaller radius depending on the snapshot (item 37 allows any $\vartheta_n\in[\vartheta_n^\flat,\frac14]$, and along a Case 3 sequence $(\vartheta_n^\flat)^{14}\alpha_n^7\le C(\log X_n)^2\alpha_n/X_n^2\to0$ because $\alpha_n^2=\alpha_n\nu_nX_n\le CX_n\log X_n$ by item 15, so with $\rho/\lambda=\alpha_n$ the model does not reach $\vartheta_n^\flat$ for large $n$: the probability estimate needs $\vartheta^2\rho/\lambda\ge64$, and for $\vartheta^2\rho/\lambda<1$ the velocity of the stationary field on the tube $rz=Y$ at $r=\vartheta\rho$, which is $U\lambda/(\vartheta^2\rho)$, exceeds $U$), nor a single-time estimate that uses further properties of the record solution. It shows that the instantaneous coupling, finite energy, (A), small $\alpha\nu$ and large $rM$ do not by themselves give depletion at every radius of a prescribed range $[\vartheta_0,\frac14]$, and it points to the production and transport history (the production of $\eta$ near the axis by the swirl and the history of the ejecting flow) as the natural further input. Item 33 concerns the route through alternative (I): for source-free passive data with the circulation bound at every time the logarithm can be removed for affine drifts but not for general drifts, even with the passive amplitude ratios and the Hessian ratio bounded, so the needed version of item 14 must use structure not captured by passive estimates (for instance the exact coupling $r\eta=\partial_zu^r-\partial_ru^z$ of $\eta$ to its own drift, energy constraints, or the geometry of the annulus; finite energy and the instantaneous Biot-Savart law alone do not suffice by item 18). A corresponding planar model question, a persistence bound $T\le C(c_0,C_*)\ell^2$ for two-dimensional Navier-Stokes vorticity of size $c_0D$ that is uniform in the Reynolds number $M\ell$ under $MH\le C_*D^2$, is not resolved by the arguments here (compactness and the Koch-Nadirashvili-Seregin-Sverak Liouville theorem settle it for bounded Reynolds number only, and a bound $T\le C(c_0)\ell^2\log(2+M\ell)$ holds with no shape condition, 8.3 (iii)); the passive counterexample of item 33 has not been transferred to the meridional annulus with killing, and for a cell evolving by its own Proudman-Johnson equation (the coupled class of item 33) a persistence bound uniform in the initial amplitude is not decided. In the unbounded strained-shear model $u=(\gamma(t)x,-\gamma(t)y+v(x,t))$ such a bound does hold, because transverse expansion multiplies the oscillation of the velocity profile: if $\|\omega(T)\|_\infty\ge a$ then $\operatorname{osc}v(t)\ge e^{\int_0^t\gamma}a^2/\|\partial_x^2v(0)\|_\infty$ for $0\le t\le T$, hence $T\le4C_*^2\ell^2/(\pi c_0^6)$ for either sign of $\gamma$. Here the full velocity is unbounded, so $M$ denotes a bound for half the oscillation of the profile $v$ on $[0,T]$, and (A) is imposed as $M\|\partial_x^2v(0)\|_\infty\le C_*D^2$ with $\ell=M/D$ (derived from the exact solution formula; checked here and rederived in an adversarial review). For bounded flows, one proposed route would prove that the persistent backward average selects a transverse coordinate with coherent expansion over a width much larger than $\ell$, with errors uniform in the Reynolds number; persistence alone has not been shown to provide that structure, and other exclusion routes remain possible. Neither the circulation identity at density levels nor the stochastic Weber formula supplies it, and a kinematic test (not a Navier-Stokes solution) suggests that an oscillating shear along the layer can shred the expelled material into strips of width $\ell$, which avoids the fattening. As numerical evidence only, not part of any proof: a second-model pseudo-spectral study of unforced two-dimensional Navier-Stokes flow on periodic squares (51 trajectories, five families of initial data including decaying turbulence, a strained vortex, a sheet on the stable manifold of a stagnation point, a sheet with exterior jets and merging vortices, initial Reynolds numbers $30$ to $1000$, $c_0=\frac14,\frac12$, $C_*\le10$) found sampled values of $T/\ell^2$ of at most about $6.3$ and $2.2$ (the first in a run that fails the study's own spectral resolution screen; about $5.9$ among the runs passing it), close to the amplitude-independent values $5.72$ and $2.24$ of the exact Oseen vortex (with Euclidean velocity, Frobenius gradient and Hessian norms and historical suprema). The resolved strained-vortex runs do increase with the Reynolds number over the tested range (from about $4.1$ to $5.4$ for $c_0=\frac14$ and from about $1.7$ to $2.2$ for $c_0=\frac12$) and appear to flatten; a finite campaign gives no evidence either way about the limit of infinite Reynolds number. One case was reproduced here with an independent solver. On a periodic square of side $2\pi L$ the energy identity and the spectral gap give $T/\ell^2\le\frac12(L/\ell)^2\log(1+128\pi C_*^2/c_0^4)$, so such tests cannot detect growth that needs box-to-core ratios $L/\ell$ larger than those simulated (about $1.2$ to $10.3$ at the sampled maximizing windows).
3. **The tested scalar comparisons give no contradiction.** Section 4.8 supplies parameter laws satisfying the comparisons involving $M,D,r,H$ and the stated scalar upper bounds for several budgets (energy, dissipation including the improved bound $r_n\alpha_n^2\to0$, the global $\eta$ source budget, the critical $L^1$ budget and the logarithmic window bound); it does not construct a joint history satisfying the actual energy, dissipation, swirl, source, adjoint and transport constraints. The vertical spread required in alternative (I) (item 30) gives no contradiction at this level either: on the logarithmic family of Section 4.8, where $\alpha_n^2=\kappa\log(1/s)$, a layer of high vorticity with thickness $\ell_n$, radius $r_n$ and height $r_ne^{\lambda\alpha_n^2}$ has energy of order $M_n^2r_n^3e^{\lambda\alpha_n^2}/\alpha_n=s^{3c-2a-\lambda\kappa}/\alpha_n$, and, if it were maintained over the window $T_\vartheta$, a dissipation of order $M_n^2r_n^3\alpha_ne^{\lambda\alpha_n^2}$; both tend to zero, and the height stays below the transport distance $M_nT_\vartheta$, when $\lambda\kappa<\min(a-c,3c-2a)$, which for any fixed $\lambda$ holds once $\kappa$ is small. This is exponent arithmetic only; no evolution realizing such a layer is constructed.
4. **Conditional items.** The bound $c_1M_n^2/D_n\le\|\eta_0\|_{L^1}+\int_0^{t_n}\|\partial_z(q^2)\|_{L^1}$ in item 9 assumes $\eta_0\in L^1(\mathbb R^3)$; by item 22 its consequences for Case 3 (items 9, 19, 21) hold without this assumption, with the time integrals started at any fixed $s_0>0$.
5. **Dependence on published theorems.** Item 1 of Theorem 1 uses the KNSS two-dimensional Liouville theorem for bounded ancient mild solutions; the branch reduction in general (Lemma 2.2(i)) also uses their axisymmetric no-swirl theorem and the branch negation (NSE).
6. **Checked against the source; no restriction on pure power exponents.** Theorem 1.2 of [LNZ16] concerns a smooth axisymmetric solution on $\mathbb R^3\times(-T,0)$ with $v_0\in L^2$ and $rv_0^\theta\in L^\infty$. At points with $r<\min\{R,\frac12\}$ and times within $R^2$ of the final time, where $R\le1$ depends only on $T$, it gives $$|\omega^\theta(x,t)|\le\frac{C\log(1/r)}{r^{7/2}}\Big[\Big(\sup_{s\in[t-r^2,t]}\int_{B(x,4r)}\big((u^r)^2+(u^z)^2\big)(y,s)\,dy\Big)^{1/2}+r^{1/2}(G+1)\Big]^2\Big[\Big(\int_{t-r^2}^t\int_{B(x,4r)}(\omega^\theta)^2\,dy\,ds\Big)^{1/2}+r^{1/2}(G+1)\Big],$$ with $C$ depending only on the data. After the time shift $t\mapsto t-T_*$ the hypotheses hold for the solution of 1.1 (smoothness is needed only on the open interval). Apply the theorem at the point $x^0$ of the preamble of Section 4.7, where $|\omega^\theta(x^0,t_n)|\ge\frac34\kappa_0D_n$ and $|r^0-r_n|\le R_0r_n/\alpha_n$; the theorem bounds only $\omega^\theta$, which suffices there. The local energy integral is at most $E_0$. The local enstrophy factor tends to zero uniformly in $x$ and $t$ as $r\to0$, because $|\omega|^2\le2|\nabla u|^2$ and $t\mapsto\|\nabla u(t)\|_2^2$ is integrable on $(0,T_*)$. Together these give $D_nr_n^{7/2}/\log(1/r_n)\to0$. Items 4 and 15 of Theorem 1 give the weaker bound $D_nr_n^{7/2}\le C\log^2(r_nM_n)$: item 4 gives $M_n^6\le C(D_n^2/r_n)^{3/2}$, and item 15 gives $D_n^4\le C\log^2(r_nM_n)M_n^6/r_n^2$. Hence for pure power laws $M\asymp s^{-a}$, $D\asymp s^{-b}$, $r\asymp s^c$ ($s\downarrow0$) items 4 and 15 already force $b\le\frac72c$, so the new condition restricts no exponents; it can exclude logarithmic corrections (for instance $M=r^{-2}L$, $D=r^{-7/2}L^2$, $L=\log(1/r)$, satisfies items 4 and 15 as scalar relations but not this condition). Both scalar tests of 8.2 satisfy it ($D_nr_n^{7/2}=M_n^{-9/10}$ and $Dr^{7/2}=s^{21/40}$); this says nothing about their realization by a solution. The condition is not used in any proof.
7. **The swirl source.** Item 19 reduces the critical swirl source budget to an inflow-weighted critical swirl integral; with trivial bounds that integral is only $\lesssim E_0D_n$, far larger than $1/\nu_n$, so no contradiction follows. One formal proposal is a swirl-generated pair of opposite-sign meridional cores (the source, a $z$-derivative, creates opposite-sign meridional circulation in pairs) formed near the axis and subsequently transported outward, the inner core thinning by stretching; only the stated scalar comparisons have been checked for it, without source sign sorting, adjoint weights or retention histories. Possible exclusion routes include a retention estimate (the one-sign circulation that can be assembled into the record core is $o(1/\nu_n)$ despite the dipolar source), a bound $S(t_n)=O(1/\nu_n)$ (which contradicts the proved divergence $S(t_n)\nu_n\to\infty$ of Lemmas 7.25(c) and 7.26(c)), an upper bound $o(1/\nu_n)$ for the critical inflow integral from a fixed time $s_0$ (which contradicts its proved lower bound; the available bound $O(E_0D_n)$ does not), or a local energy-flux bound for the core, which would still have to be formulated at a size that contradicts the record cost; none is proved here. A refined formal multi-cell proposal with $1/\nu_n$ small swirl cells near the axis matches the projected parameter sizes checked against the $L^1$, $L^p$ and critical swirl estimates of items 19 and 21-23; it does not supply the source, sign separation, retention, adjoint correlation, merging or energy-supply histories needed to verify the corresponding integral budgets (the adjoint requirement of item 24 is not verified for it). These parameter comparisons produce no contradiction, but they do not establish compatibility of a completed configuration with the full necessary conditions. Mechanisms that were considered but not turned into proofs include sign-selective transport of the swirl-produced circulation, the energy supply of an expanding core, a Hou-Li type maximum principle, and the conservation of circulation within bands of $\Gamma$. These approaches are not developed here into complete proofs of exclusion; none is used to establish a numbered result, and no conclusion about Case 3 is based on auxiliary calculations made while examining them.
8. **No counterexample.** No fixed-datum singular solution realizing Case 3 is constructed here, and the compatibility test of Section 4.8 (subsection 8.2) is not such a construction. The inspected numerical fits do not exhibit Case 3, and they prove nothing. Hou and Huang [HH21] report numerical evidence, for the axisymmetric Euler equations with degenerate variable viscosity coefficients, of a potential singularity whose fitted ring radius scales like $(T-t)^{1/2}$ and whose fitted ring thickness scales like $T-t$, so that in their similarity model the ratio of the distance to the axis to the local scale grows like $(T-t)^{-1/2}$, as in Case 3. Their fitted rates $\|u\|_\infty\sim(T-t)^{-1/2}$ and $\|\omega\|_\infty\sim(T-t)^{-3/2}$ would give, at any fixed positive viscosity, $\nu=D/M^2\gtrsim(T-t)^{-1/2}\to\infty$ rather than $\nu\to0$ (a conditional scaling computation; their viscosity is not constant), see Section 5. Their computations with constant viscosity between $10^{-7}$ and $10^{-4}$ did not exhibit a finite-time singularity from the same data and suppressed the two-scale structure over the resolved evolution; this is numerical evidence, not a regularity result. The potentially singular Navier-Stokes computation of [Hou21] (in a cylinder, periodic in $z$, with a no-slip wall at $r=1$, and with the viscosity changed once during the run) is described by its author as essentially one-scale: the distance $R(t)$ of the maximum of $u^\theta/r$ to the axis and the vertical scale $Z(t)$ are both fitted as $(T-t)^{1/2}$, with $R/Z$ decaying most of the time. These leading similarity fits do not exhibit the simultaneous limits $\alpha_n\to\infty$, $\nu_n\to0$ required here, but the fitted location and width have not been identified with our gradient-record radius and scale, logarithmic corrections are not controlled (with the refined fit $\|\omega\|_\infty\sim|\log(T-t)|/(T-t)$ and $D\asymp\|\omega\|_\infty$ one would get $R/\ell\asymp|\log(T-t)|$ and $\nu\asymp|\log(T-t)|$), and the computations do not exclude later unresolved behaviour. The author cautions that the fitted rate $\|u\|_\infty\sim(T-t)^{-1/2}$ is not a uniform bound; in $\mathbb R^3$ an exact Type I bound $\|u(t)\|_\infty\le C(T-t)^{-1/2}$ excludes singularities of axisymmetric strong solutions with $H^{1/2}$ data and bounded $ru^\theta$ [CSTY09, Theorem 1.1], a whole-space result that does not apply directly to the cylinder. (That theorem also assumes $p\in L^{5/3}_{t,x}$; for the canonical pressure of the finite-energy solutions considered here this holds, since $\int_0^{T_*}\|u\|_{10/3}^{10/3}dt\le C\sup_t\|u\|_2^{4/3}\int_0^{T_*}\|\nabla u\|_2^2dt<\infty$ and the Calderon-Zygmund operators are bounded on $L^{5/3}$.)

9. **Further necessary conditions examined; none closes the argument.** (a) Outward strain seen by the ancestors of the record. Testing the five-dimensional backward evolution of the record cutoff against $r^{-3/2}$ (for which $\Delta_5r^{-3/2}=-\frac34r^{-7/2}$, with no mass on the axis, so that the backward process started at radius $r_0$ satisfies $\int\mathbb E R_h^{-7/2}\,dh\le\frac43r_0^{-3/2}e^{3K_+/2}$, $K_+$ the integrated supremum of $(u^r/r)_+$) and bounding the swirl source by $|\partial_z(q^2)|\le4G^{3/2}H^{1/2}r^{-7/2}$ gives $\int_{s_0}^{t_n}\|(u^r/r)_+\|_\infty ds\ge\frac13\log(r_nM_n)-C$. This is of the same kind as item 20 and is not a contradiction, since no upper bound for such time integrals of a supremum is known. (b) Replacing the supremum of the outward strain by its energy, $\|u^r/r\|_2\le\|\nabla u\|_2$, and using the pointwise decay of the backward density removes the exponential factor and gives the power-law conditions $M_n^{13}\le CD_n^{10}r_n$ and $M_n^6\le CD_n^5r_n$; both follow directly from $\alpha_n\to\infty$, $M_n,D_n\to\infty$ and (E'), since $M_n^{13}/(D_n^{10}r_n)=(M_n^5/D_n^3)^3/(\alpha_nM_n^3)\to0$ and $M_n^6/(D_n^5r_n)=(M_n^5/D_n^3)/(\alpha_nD_n)\to0$, so they exclude nothing (their derivation is not reproduced here). (c) Bounding the adjoint-weighted quartic swirl integral $\iint q^4\psi$ by the swirl maximum and the dissipation weighted by the backward density, and combining this with the entropy form of the duality, gives $S_n^4\varepsilon_n\gtrsim M_n^2/(r_nL_n)$. Here $\psi_n$ stands for a backward weight of the record (the backward evolution of a bounded terminal cutoff of the record disk, which is bounded by $1$; a point-source transition density is a different object and need not be), $\varepsilon_n=\int\!\!\int\psi_n|\nabla u|^2\,dx\,ds$ for the dissipation weighted by it over the history, and $L_n$ for a logarithmic factor produced by the entropy form of the duality; the precise weight, interval, normalization and logarithm, and the derivation, are not supplied here, so this is a proposed comparison and not a proved statement. In exponent form (logarithms dropped, swirl exponent $\sigma_{\max}=(2b-a)/3$, the largest allowed by restriction (i) of 8.1, and the favourable dissipation exponent $p=\max(\frac23(c+2b-2a)-1,0)$ for $\varepsilon_n\lesssim s^p$) compatibility requires $4\sigma_{\max}-p\ge2a+c$; this fails on part of the exponent region of Section 4.8 (for instance at $(a,b,c)=(\frac85,\frac{14}5,1)$, where $4\sigma_{\max}-p=\frac{61}{15}<\frac{63}{15}=2a+c$), but only where $p>0$ and $4b+3<6a+5c$ (for $p=0$ it reads $8b\ge10a+3c$, which follows from $b>a+c$ and $2c>a$; for $p>0$ it reads $4b+3\ge6a+5c$), and with $b>a+c$ this forces $a\ge1$ or $b\ge2$ (if $a<1$ and $b<2$ then $c<b-a<2-a$, so $2a+c<a+2<3$ and $4b+3>4a+4c+3>6a+5c$). So, with the maximal swirl exponent and the favourable dissipation exponent assigned above, the exponent test of the proposed comparison excludes no point that satisfies the condition $1+c-2a>0$ of Section 8.2 (which implies $a<1$ and $b<2$); a proof of the comparison could still constrain histories whose swirl or weighted dissipation lies below these assignments. It also holds on the formal history: with swirl $\|u^\theta\|_\infty=s^{-3/5}$ and $p=\frac7{30}$ the exponent of the left side is smaller than that of the right side by $\frac7{60}$ (by $\frac{13}{150}$ under the further assignment $\|\nabla u\|_2^2=s^{-97/100}$ with the ceiling $\varepsilon_n\lesssim s^{79/300}$, an upper ceiling that the actual weighted dissipation need not attain). Either a swirl bound below the axis cap on the support of the backward weight or a rate for $\varepsilon_n$ could potentially turn it into an exclusion; neither is available.

10. **Where produced vorticity goes: inflow annihilation, sign selection, and the formal scenario an exclusion must defeat.** Each statement below carries its own status label: analytic claims are identified individually as proved here, stated without proof, or conditional model calculations, prescribed-drift model results are labelled as such, and the numerical observations quoted are exploratory and enter no proof. None excludes Case 3, and none is used in the proofs of Sections 3 and 4. (a) *Inflow annihilation* (proved; checked by hand and by an independent audit). Write $L=\partial_{rr}-\frac1r\partial_r+\partial_{zz}$, the adjoint of $\Delta_5$ with respect to $r\,dr\,dz$; in the variable $Z=r^2$ its radial part is $4Z\partial_{ZZ}$, the generator of a squared Bessel process of dimension $0$, which is absorbed at the axis. Let $\Omega=\{r<\rho\}\times I$ and suppose $u^r/r\le-k\le0$ on $\Omega\times[s,t]$. Let $P_\Omega(t,s)$ be the evolution of the homogeneous $\eta$ equation on $\Omega$ with zero data on the lateral boundary and on the end disks. Then $\int_\Omega|P_\Omega(t,s)\eta|\,dx\le\int_\Omega|\eta(y)|\,\Phi_k(r_y,t-s)\,dy$ with $\Phi_k(r,T)=1-\exp\big(-kr^2/(2(e^{2kT}-1))\big)\le\min\{r^2/(4T),\ kr^2e^{-2kT}/(2(1-e^{-2kT}))\}$. The proof is a comparison: $\Phi_k$ is the survival probability of the Feller diffusion $dZ=-2kZ\,d\tau+2\sqrt{2Z}\,dW$ and solves its backward equation exactly; for the true drift the defect is $-(k+u^r/r)\,r\partial_r\Phi_k\ge0$ because $\partial_r\Phi_k\ge0$. So the weight of $\eta$-mass kept at radius $r$ inside a cylinder with inflow of strain $k$ is at most $kr^2e^{-2kT}/(2(1-e^{-2kT}))$ after a time $T$; it is small only once $e^{2kT}\gg kr^2$, so the time after which it is small depends on $kr^2$ and is not uniform. With $k=0$ the weight is $1-e^{-r^2/(4T)}\le r^2/(4T)$, which suppresses only mass at radii $r\ll T^{1/2}$. For the stagnation flow $u^r=-kr$, $u^z=2kz$ on the whole half-plane $\{r>0\}$, with absorption only at the axis and nonnegative data, $\Phi_k$ is the exact surviving weight; on the killed finite cylinder the bound is a survival bound, not an identity. Here $r$ is the radius at which the mass starts, not the location of the surviving mass. The corresponding axis-survival upper bound under bounded outflow $u^r/r\le k'$ holds with $1-\exp\big(-k'r^2/(2(1-e^{-2k'T}))\big)$. The mass balance of an inflow cylinder then reads: present mass is at most the old mass weighted by $\Phi_k$, plus the production weighted by $\Phi_k$, plus the mass entering through the boundary. (b) *Use for alternative (II).* In alternative (II) a net signed contribution to the stopped representation of the record comes from the boundary $r=\vartheta r_n$ within the window of Theorem 7.12; this does not identify material crossing the interior cylinder. If that cylinder carries inflow of strain $k$ over the window, of length $T$, then (a) bounds the inherited mass by its $\Phi_k$-weighted value, which is small only where $e^{2kT}\gg kr^2$; a strain of the order of the reciprocal of the window length does not suffice (for $kT=1$ and $kr^2=100$ one has $\Phi_k\approx0.9996$). Even where the inherited part is small, the conclusion that the record material is produced inside the window would need in addition a quantitative bound, smaller than the required record contribution, on the mass entering through the boundary of the cylinder; moreover the contribution in Theorem 7.12 is a net contribution of the stochastic representation, not an amount of identified material. Neither step is established here. If the cylinder does not carry such inflow throughout the window but satisfies $u^r/r\le0$ on it (failure of a uniform negative bound does not imply this), then (a) with $k=0$ suppresses only mass at radii much smaller than the square root of the window length, a small fraction of the cylinder radius; where $u^r/r>0$ somewhere in the cylinder (outflow or a focusing drift, $u^r/r>0$ inside, $<0$ outside) only the bounded-outflow bound of (a) applies, which for $u^r/r\le k'$ with $k'>0$ does not tend to zero as the window grows (its limit is $1-e^{-k'r^2/2}$), so it gives no annihilation; whether storage over longer times needs such a drift is not proved. Neither alternative is excluded by the scalar comparisons. (c) *Sign selection* (formula proved for the exact flow; consequences numerical). The source $\partial_z(q^2)$ integrates to zero in $z$; it is positive on the rising flank and negative on the falling flank of an axial maximum of $q^2$. In a stagnation cell $u^r=-kr$, $u^z=2kz$, $|z|<L$, for $k>0$ a particle starting at height $z_0$ with $0<|z_0|<L$ leaves the cell at time $\tau$ with $e^{2k\tau}-1=(L-|z_0|)/|z_0|$. Neglecting axial diffusion and evaluating the radial survival at this deterministic exit age, the fraction of the vorticity produced by a Burgers column ($q_*=Gk/2$, core radius $(2/k)^{1/2}$) at relative height $u=|z_0|/L$ that survives to the end disks is proportional to $j(u)=2\log2-\frac{u\log u+(2-u)\log(2-u)}{1-u}$, with $j(0)=0$, $j(1)=2\log2$ and $j(1/4)/j(3/4)\approx0.479$. The normalized quantity $j(u)/(2\log2)$ is a diagnostic, not the exact survival fraction of the viscous problem in the finite cell: with axial diffusion the exit time is random, and for $z_0=0$ the exact surviving fraction is positive. For the profile $\Gamma=G(1-e^{-kr^2/2})\chi(z)$ with a unit axial hump $\chi$ (one flank rising from $0$ to $1$ and one falling back to $0$), the positive and the negative $\eta$-source masses, measured with $dx=2\pi r\,dr\,dz$, are each $\pi G^2k\log2$ per unit time, from $\int_0^\infty((1-e^{-x})/x)^2dx=2\log2$; the meridional circulation produced, measured with $dr\,dz$, is smaller by the factor $2\pi$, and a column independent of $z$ produces nothing. Hence, in this diagnostic, the half of a production dipole that lies closer to an inflow stagnation plane is annihilated preferentially; selection is complete only in the limit of long inflow stretches. Typical survivors leave at radius of order $(2/k)^{1/2}$, the conditional-on-survival radial scale of the cell, not at the deterministically compressed radius (a statement about the distribution of exit radii after times long compared with $1/k$, not about every trajectory). (d) *Arbitrary high points* (proved by inspection of the proofs of Theorems 7.12 and 7.18). Fix $\beta\in(0,1]$, $\vartheta_0\in(0,\frac14]$ and $R_0\ge1$. Let $t'\le t_n$ and $x'=(r',z')$ with $\vartheta_0r_n\le r'\le R_0r_n$ and $a'=|\eta(x',t')|\ge\beta a_n$. Then Theorem 7.12 holds at $(x',t')$ in place of $(x^0,t_n)$, with $a'$ in place of $a_n$, the constants $\epsilon\beta\vartheta_0$ and $L/(\beta\vartheta_0)$ in place of $\epsilon$ and $L$, the annulus $\{\vartheta r'<r<Lr'/(\beta\vartheta_0)\}$ and the horizon $T'=\epsilon\beta\vartheta_0\vartheta^3r'^2/(1+G)$; in the transport alternative the strain clock carried out from $r=\vartheta r'$ is $\log(1/\vartheta)$ up to an error that vanishes only as $\vartheta\to0$. Theorem 7.18 holds at $(x',t')$, for all large $n$, every $\vartheta\in(0,\frac14]$ and every such $(x',t')$ with $T'<t'$ and $\vartheta^3r'M_n\ge C_1'=(1+G)/(\epsilon\beta\vartheta_0)$: if the net inner-boundary contribution $J'(\vartheta)=\sup_{0<h\le T'}\big(\mathbb E[\operatorname{sgn}\eta(x',t')\,\eta(X_\tau,t'-\tau);\tau\le h,\ R_\tau=\vartheta r']\big)_+$ (backward process from $(x',t')$, $\tau$ its exit time from the annulus) is at most $a'/4$, then $\alpha_n^2(r'/r_n)^4\le\frac{C_I}{\beta^3\vartheta_0}\vartheta^{-5}\log\big(12C_N(L')^4r'M_n/\vartheta^3\big)$ with $L'=L/(\beta\vartheta_0)$ and $C_I$ as in Theorem 7.18 (from $a'^2\le\frac{18e(1+G)M_n^2}{\epsilon\beta\vartheta_0\vartheta^5r'^4}\log(12C_N(L')^4r'M_n/\vartheta^3)$ and $a'\ge3\beta\kappa_0D_n/(8r_n)$); the killed persistence obtained is $a'/2$ and the transferred vorticity bound is $\vartheta r'a'/2$, so the record-specific constants of Theorem 7.18 do not carry over unchanged. The proofs use only the stopped representation at the chosen point and time, the bound $D(s)\le D_n$ for $s\le t_n$ (which gives the swirl-source bound $2GD_n/(\vartheta r')^3$ on the annulus and the outer-boundary bound $\sqrt2D_n\beta\vartheta_0/(Lr')$), the velocity bound $M(s)\le M_n$, and the value $a'$; the new constants make the source and outer terms sum to at most $a'/4$ exactly as in the original choice, and nothing uses that $(x',t')$ is a record. Iterating this toward the axis is not justified: the condition at each step carries the factor $\vartheta^{-5}$ (a fifth-power condition), the amplitude is lost by a factor up to $4$ per step, and clocks over overlapping windows do not add. Below the smallest admissible radius the elementary pointwise bounds are $|\omega^\theta|\le\sqrt2\min(D_n,H_nr)$ and $|\omega|\le\sqrt2D_n$; these bounds alone do not exclude a reservoir at smaller radii. (e) *Formal scenario* (heuristic, not a construction). Assemble (a) to (d), the formal scalar history of Section 4.8 and the window-local geometry in which a thin ring is stretched in the outflow ahead of a larger ring (which numerically satisfies (A) with a bounded constant, unlike a head-on collision of two thin rings). This gives a sign-consistent fountain. A chain of co-moving positive rings (a positive ring moves in $+z$, with axial inflow behind it and outflow ahead) holds swirl caps at the inflow stagnation planes between them. The negative half of each cap's production dipole is annihilated at the axis, and the positive half is ejected radially and feeds a new ring. This picture is not excluded by the scalar estimates displayed in Section 4.8 or by the sampled exponent comparisons of item (f); its production, sign selection, replenishment and retention are not constructed and remain conditional. Its caps carry swirl of order $g/\ell_n=(T_*-t)^{-13/20}$ near the axis (fixed amplitude $g$, core size $\ell_n$), a different augmented swirl history from the one with $\|u^\theta\|_\infty=s^{-3/5}$ used in item 9(c); both lie below the axis cap of restriction (i) of 8.1; at fixed assigned $\varepsilon_n,L_n$ the larger swirl makes the proposed scalar inequality of 9(c) easier to satisfy, and no corresponding coupled history is constructed. Its weakest points are quantitative: the energy supply of the ejected ring, which must reach the large-ring circulation (of order $\alpha_n$ times the record circulation $M_n\ell_n$) required by the stretching geometry; the completeness of the wrong-sign annihilation; and the regeneration at ever smaller radii required by later records. Numerical runs at moderate parameters show the spatial sorting of the two signs at an inflow plane and a balanced, expelled pair at an outflow plane. They show incomplete annihilation of the wrong sign and no growth of the self-compression. In one such run (Reynolds number 4000, a swirl cap behind a ring), the cap was held in a single episode with time-integrated axial inflow strain about $0.6$ and constant swirl amplitude, and the gross production agreed within 4 percent with the per-sign formula of item (c) integrated along the moving cap; the later gradient maximum consisted of material produced in this one episode and was not replenished, its vorticity stayed between $0.93$ and $1.38$ times the gradient maximum, and the ratio of its radius to the core size grew about tenfold, mainly because the ring expanded at a nearly fixed core scale (so this is a persistent thin ring, not an approach to the axis). These are exploratory axisymmetric Navier-Stokes computations at moderate Reynolds numbers, and they enter no proof. Specifications: the system was solved for $\omega^\theta$ and $\Gamma$ on a rectangle $0\le r\le R_{\max}$, $|z|\le L_z$ with a uniform grid of spacing $h$ and fourth-order centred differences; the stream function has zero Dirichlet data on all four sides (impermeable outer wall and end planes), $\Gamma=0$ on the axis and on the outer wall, and $\omega^\theta$ is odd about every boundary. The sorting runs (viscosity $\nu=5\cdot10^{-4}$, Reynolds number $1/\nu=2000$) started from two Gaussian rings $\omega^\theta=(\pi a^2)^{-1}e^{-((r-1)^2+(z\mp1.5)^2)/a^2}$, $a=0.3$, and a swirl column $\Gamma_0=0.05(1-e^{-r^2/0.08^2})e^{-(z-z_s)^2/0.3^2}$ placed at the inflow plane ($z_s=0.5$) or at the outflow plane ($z_s=-0.5$) of the relative axial flow, with $R_{\max}=2$, $L_z=3.5$, $h=0.0125$. The cap runs ($\nu=2.5\cdot10^{-4}$, Reynolds number $1/\nu=4000$) started from one Gaussian ring of circulation 2, core radius 0.3 and radius 1 at $z=0$ and the column $\Gamma_0=0.05(1-e^{-r^2/0.08^2})e^{-(z+1.5)^2/4}$, whose front part sits in the ring's rear inflow, with $(R_{\max},L_z)$ equal to $(2,4)$, $(4,5)$ and $(4,8)$ and $h=0.0125$ (24 cells per ring core radius, 6.4 per column radius). The produced thin ring that becomes the gradient maximum has an effective core radius of about 5 grid cells, which is marginal; one cap run (on the domain $(R_{\max},L_z)=(2,4)$, up to time 5) was repeated at half the grid spacing. In that comparison the time-integrated quantities (the production totals by sign, the axis fluxes by sign and the surviving produced mass) agreed within 1 percent, and the gradient maximum within 1 to 2 percent up to time 3 and within 6 percent at times 4 to 5, while pointwise quantities at individual times agreed less well: the cap strain differed by up to about 25 percent (23 percent at time 3), and the positions of the gradient maximum at time 4 differed by more than one grid spacing. The Hessian maximum, after an error in the evaluation of this diagnostic at the axis had been corrected (the evolution itself was not affected), agreed between the two grids at time 0; at time 5 it lay on the produced ring in both runs, with values differing by about 30 percent, and the corresponding shape ratio $MH/D^2$ was about $2.3$ and $2.6$. On the coarser grid the ratio formed with the historical suprema stayed below about $2.5$ up to time 5. Since the core of the produced ring spans only 4.5 to 5 grid cells, these values are indications, not converged measurements. The longer runs on the larger domains were not refined, and the last 10 to 20 percent of the shorter runs is influenced by the end walls. An exclusion of Case 3 has to defeat this scenario with information that the scalar histories do not encode. (f) *Localization of the quartic swirl condition* (exponent comparisons on a grid; not a theorem). The quartic swirl-entropy condition of item 9(c), with the swirl kept inside the dissipation integral, splits at $r=\vartheta r_n$. In the part from $r>\vartheta r_n$ the swirl is at most $G/(\vartheta r_n)$, so that part is at most $G^4(\vartheta r_n)^{-4}\varepsilon_n$. With power laws $M=s^{-a}$, $D=s^{-b}$, $r=s^{c}$ and the most favourable dissipation exponent $p=\max(\frac23(c+2b-2a)-1,0)$ for $\varepsilon_n\lesssim s^{p}$, this part alone can satisfy the condition only if $p\le3c-2a$ (logarithms and $\vartheta$ dropped). Here the weighted inequality with the swirl inside is itself only proposed, and $p$ is a limiting favourable scalar choice that energy integrability alone does not supply. The outer part is then at most $Cs^{p-4c}$ against the required $s^{-2a-c}/L$, and the condition $p\le3c-2a$ fails exactly when $3c<2a$ or $4b+2a-7c>3$. On the points $(a,b,c)=(i,j,k)/60$, $1\le i\le239$, $1\le j\le359$, $1\le k\le179$, of the admissible exponent region of Section 4.8 (alternative (I) not imposed) it fails at 109426 of 109964 points (at 281 of the 819 points that satisfy the condition $1+c-2a>0$ of Section 8.2), and on the formal history (where $p-(3c-2a)=\frac1{12}$). These sampled comparisons, under the proposed inequality, suggest that on much of the region the ancestors of a record collect their swirl dissipation close to the axis; a localization theorem would need the region where the inequality fails described exactly and a proof there. The part from $r<\vartheta r_n$ reduces to an outward-strain clock of the type in item 9(a), which the formal history satisfies. (g) *Inter-generation accounting* (proved; checked by hand). For every $\lambda>0$ and $t_0<t<T_*$, $$\int(\eta(t)-\lambda)_+dx+4\pi\int_{t_0}^t\!\!\int(\eta(0,z,s)-\lambda)_+dz\,ds\le\int(\eta(t_0)-\lambda)_+dx+\int_{t_0}^t\!\!\int_{\{\eta(s)>\lambda\}}\partial_z(q^2)\,dx\,ds,$$ and the same holds for $-\eta$ with the source $-\partial_z(q^2)$. Proof: multiply the $\eta$ equation by $\phi_\varepsilon'(\eta-\lambda)$, where $\phi_\varepsilon$ is convex, vanishes on $(-\infty,0]$, $0\le\phi_\varepsilon'\le1$ and $\phi_\varepsilon'\to1_{(0,\infty)}$, integrate over $\mathbb R^3$ (finiteness of the integrals and the limit passages follow from $u\in C([0,T_*);H^k)$ with a spatial cutoff, as in the proof of Lemma 7.7), and let $\varepsilon\to0$. The transport term vanishes because $b$ is divergence free in $\mathbb R^3$. Write $\Delta_5=\Delta_3+\frac2r\partial_r$: the $\Delta_3$ part gives $-\int\phi_\varepsilon''|\nabla\eta|^2dx\le0$ (Kato's inequality; this is the only place where an inequality enters), and $\int1_{\{\eta>\lambda\}}\frac2r\partial_r\eta\,dx=4\pi\iint\partial_r(\eta-\lambda)_+\,dr\,dz=-4\pi\int(\eta(0,z)-\lambda)_+dz$. For almost every $\lambda$ the production term equals $\int_{\{\eta=\lambda\}}q^2\nu_z\,dS$, where $\nu$ is the outer normal of $\{\eta>\lambda\}$. Two pointwise facts locate the super-level sets: $|\eta(t)|\le\sup|\partial_r\omega^\theta(t)|\le\sqrt2H(t)$, because $\omega^\theta$ vanishes on the axis, and $|\omega^\theta|\le\sqrt2D(t)$, so $\{|\eta(t)|>\lambda\}\subset\{r<\sqrt2D(t)/\lambda\}$. Apply this at a Case 3 record. By the proof of Lemma 7.8, $\omega^\theta(t_n)$ has one sign and modulus at least $\frac38\kappa_0D_n$ on a meridional disk of radius $\rho_1=3\kappa_0\ell_n/(8\sqrt2C_*)$ about a point $(r^0,z^0)$ with $r^0+\rho_1\le2r_n$ for large $n$.
