Take that sign and $\lambda_n=\frac3{16}\kappa_0D_n/(r^0+\rho_1)\ge\frac3{32}\kappa_0D_n/r_n$; then $\int(\pm\eta(t_n)-\lambda_n)_+dx=2\pi\iint(\pm\omega^\theta-\lambda_nr)_+\,dr\,dz\ge c_1/(2\nu_n)$ with the constant $c_1$ of Lemma 7.8. Let $\tau_n$ be the last time before $t_n$ with $\sqrt2H(\tau_n)\le\frac3{32}\kappa_0D_n/r_n$ (the set of such times is closed because $H$ is nondecreasing and lower semicontinuous, and nonempty for large $n$ because $H$ is bounded on an initial interval and $D_n/r_n\to\infty$; $\tau_n<t_n$ because $|\eta(t_n)|\ge2\lambda_n$ somewhere). Then $\int(\pm\eta(\tau_n)-\lambda_n)_+dx=0$, hence $$\int_{\tau_n}^{t_n}\!\!\int_{\{\pm\eta>\lambda_n\}}\pm\partial_z(q^2)\,dx\,dt\ \ge\ \frac{c_1}{2\nu_n}+4\pi\int_{\tau_n}^{t_n}\!\!\int(\pm\eta(0,z,t)-\lambda_n)_+dz\,dt,$$ and the super-level sets that receive this production lie in $\{r<\frac{32\sqrt2}{3\kappa_0}r_n\}$. In words: the excess of the record core over the level $\lambda_n$ is produced by the swirl source after $\tau_n$ (net signed production, with the negative source contributions retained), inside a cylinder of a fixed multiple of the record radius, and every amount of it that reaches the axis is lost. Vorticity present at time $\tau_n$ contributes at most $\lambda_n$ to $\eta$ at later times (maximum principle for the homogeneous part), so it can remain in the core but not in the excess; the statement does not identify material trajectories. Vorticity of an earlier generation close to the axis can exceed the level $\lambda_{n'}$ of that generation (the bound is $\sqrt2H$, not the record level), so the statement is about the times $\tau_n$, not about generations. This is the budget of item 22 of Theorem 1 in Section 1.2 with three elementary refinements: the starting time is the last time at which the a priori bound $|\eta|\le\sqrt2H$ guarantees zero initial excess at the conservative threshold $\frac3{32}\kappa_0D_n/r_n$ (item 22 starts at a fixed time), the production is restricted to the super-level set and its radial location is bounded, and the loss at the axis is kept. It excludes nothing by itself: on the formal history of Section 4.8 one has $H=(T_*-t)^{-41/20}$ and $D_n/r_n=(T_*-t_n)^{-39/20}$, so $T_*-\tau_n\asymp(T_*-t_n)^{39/41}$, much longer than $T_*-t_n$. A Burgers-shaped cap of fixed amplitude $g$ and strain $k$ has Hessian of order $gk^{3/2}$, so (A) allows such a cap only for $k\lesssim k_{\max}=(H/g)^{2/3}$, which is at most a constant times $(T_*-t)^{-41/30}$ on the formal history; this is a property of the cap profile, not a bound on arbitrary strain, and $k_{\max}$ is not the holder strain $\ell_n^{-2}$ of item (h). On the saturated formal history with this cap profile, the integral of $g^2k_{\max}$ over $[\tau_n,t_n]$ is of order $(T_*-t_n)^{-11/30}$ and exceeds $1/\nu_n=(T_*-t_n)^{-1/10}$ by the factor $(T_*-t_n)^{-4/15}$; this is an upper capacity, not an actual production, selection or retention history. Per viscous core time $\ell_n^2$ the capacity of one maintained cap at $k_{\max}$ is at most a constant times $g^{4/3}\nu_n^{1/3}$ times the record circulation (using $H\lesssim\nu^{-1}\ell^{-3}$, which is (A)), and per gradient time $1/D_n=\nu_n\ell_n^2$ at most a constant times $g^{4/3}\nu_n^{4/3}$ (the radial Hessian cost alone does not establish maintained production), so a record needs either many caps or many core times, which the window $[\tau_n,t_n]$ allows. On the formal history, vorticity rings of earlier generations are too weak and too far from the axis to act as the ejectors of later generations (a scalar comparison, heuristic). (h) *Holding a cap* (proved, elementary; the last part heuristic). The inflow strain on the axis, $k(a,t)=-\lim_{r\downarrow0}u^r(r,a,t)/r$, is given by the exact kernel $k(a)=\frac34\iint\rho^2(\zeta-a)\,\omega^\theta(\rho,\zeta)\,(\rho^2+(\zeta-a)^2)^{-5/2}\,d\rho\,d\zeta$, obtained by differentiating the axis velocity $u^z(0,a)=\frac12\iint\rho^2\omega^\theta(\rho,\zeta)(\rho^2+(\zeta-a)^2)^{-3/2}\,d\rho\,d\zeta$ and using $\partial_au^z(0,a)=2k(a)$. Positive vorticity ahead of an axial point produces inflow there, positive vorticity behind it outflow; a nonnegative holder of circulation $\mathcal C_h$ at distance at least $R$ from the point gives $k\le3\mathcal C_h/(4R^2)$ (here $R$ is the distance from the point; for a thin ring the sharp constant is $1/(2\sqrt3)$, attained at axial offset $R/\sqrt3$ and ring radius $\sqrt{2/3}\,R$). Holding geometry alone gives no bound on the duration of a cap: Hill's spherical vortex ($\eta=A$ in a ball of radius $R$ translating with $U=\frac2{15}AR^2$, an exact Euler solution without swirl) keeps an inflow stagnation point at its rear for all time in its translating frame, with $k=3U/(2R)$, circulation $\mathcal C_h=5UR$ and impulse $2UR^3$ (checked numerically); this does not establish indefinite retention of a viscous swirl cap. In an exploratory viscous computation (a smoothed Hill vortex with $U=\frac12$, $R=1$, swirl of amplitude $0.05$ confined to its interior, holder Reynolds numbers $\mathcal C_h/\nu=625$, $2500$ and $10000$, followed up to time $16$, about eight travel times $R/U$, before the holder approached the end of the computational domain), the interior swirl was first carried forward along the axis to the front, so no cap was held in this first phase. At the two larger Reynolds numbers the swirl that returned around the recirculation re-formed a cap behind the centroid, which stayed held to the end of the clean part of the run, with axial inflow strain between about one half and nine tenths of the strain at the rear stagnation point. The production of this second phase came as a single pulse, distributed along the axis segment inside the holder rather than concentrated at the cap: its rate rose for about six time units and then fell to about a third of its peak, while the returning swirl spread and the swirl content inside the holder decreased. The produced vorticity, whose meridional circulation per sign reached about one seventh of that of the holder by time 16 at $\mathcal C_h/\nu=2500$ (at $10000$ the surviving produced vorticity of both signs carried about one fifth of the holder's circulation in absolute value), did not cancel the holding strain. At the smallest Reynolds number most of the interior swirl was lost within two turnovers and the second phase was weak. The total production per sign up to time 16 was about $0.89$, $2.2$ and $5.8$ at the three Reynolds numbers (ratios $2.5$ and $2.6$ per factor $4$ in $\mathcal C_h/\nu$). The first phase (up to time 3) was nearly independent of the Reynolds number ($0.59$, $0.83$, $0.94$), while the second phase (times 5 to 16: $0.25$, $1.3$, $4.8$) grew about linearly and then slightly less than linearly; it followed the fraction of the interior swirl that survived the first recirculation (about $0.50$, $0.76$, $0.90$ at time 5). At the largest Reynolds number the second phase was again a single pulse, with the same timing; the cap was held from time 5 to time 16 and the produced vorticity did not weaken the holding strain. The gradient maximum of these flows was always the holder's rim or the axial swirl gradient, never a produced structure. Tracking the products of the first phase separately (at $\mathcal C_h/\nu=2500$) showed that the two phases were not assembled into one structure: at time $16$ the surviving first-phase products (total absolute circulation about $0.28$, against $\mathcal C_h=2.5$) lay in the wake, with centroid about $2.5$ behind the holder centroid and maximal vorticity about $0.05$, while the largest produced vorticity (about $0.35$) was second-phase material produced near the axis shortly before, located at the front edge of the holder, where the first-phase field was about $0.003$. The runs at the two smaller Reynolds numbers were not repeated on a finer grid; the largest was computed on a grid about one and a half times finer. Three Reynolds numbers and one swirl amplitude do not determine how the lifetime production of a holder scales, and the computation gives no bound on the duration of holding in either direction. On the formal history of Section 4.8 a holder with $U\sim M_n$ and $R\sim r_n$ has $k\sim\ell_n^{-2}$ and $\mathcal C_h\sim\alpha_n/\nu_n$, and a cap of fixed swirl amplitude $g$ whose axial front is maintained so that its production stays comparable to $g^2k$ would produce the record circulation $1/\nu_n$ in a time of order $(T_*-t_n)^{6/5}$, much shorter than $r_n^2=(T_*-t_n)^{11/10}$; over that time the holder energy $U^2R^3$, impulse $UR^3$ and viscous cost $U^2R$ times the duration (model-profile estimates) tend to zero. Maintaining the front and retaining the required sign are not established; for a single front in a pure strain, item (i) bounds the total production by a logarithm. One sufficient strategy for an exclusion along these lines would be a decomposition of the relevant production into caps together with a bound $\int_{\tau_n}^{t_n}\sum_jg_j^2k_j\,dt=o(1/\nu_n)$ over all caps $j$ (swirl amplitude $g_j$, inflow strain $k_j$), for instance $\le C(1+\log(1+\alpha_n))$; on the formal fountain this integral is of order $1/\nu_n$. Neither the decomposition nor such a bound is proved; it would have to come from the coupled viscous evolution of $\Gamma$ and the produced vorticity, and an exclusion through failed retention, sign selection or energy transfer would not need it. (i) *Swirl fronts at a cap* (the identity is exact; the model statements concern prescribed drifts; the explicit solutions are checked by substitution, while the one-front logarithmic bound and the two-sided steady estimate are stated without their proofs; the consequences for Navier-Stokes are heuristic). With $q=u^\theta/r$ and $f=\partial_zq$, $$(\partial_t+b\cdot\nabla)f=\Delta_5f-\big(\partial_zu^z+2u^r/r\big)f-(\partial_zu^r)\,\partial_rq-2q\,\partial_z(u^r/r).$$ In a pure axial stagnation flow $u^r=-k(t)r$, $u^z=2k(t)(z-a)$, with any time dependence of $k$ and any added axial translation, the last three terms vanish (the amplification of $q$ by the inflow and the axial stretching of its profile cancel), so such a flow neither creates nor amplifies axial swirl gradients and $\sup|f|$ is nonincreasing. For one swirl front, $\Gamma_0=G(1-e^{-r^2/(2w_0^2)})\chi(z-a)$ with $0\le\chi\le1$, total variation of $\chi$ at most $2$ and $|\chi'|\le1/L$, the solution is explicit (the radial factor keeps the same form with a squared width $\beta$ solving $\beta'=2-2k\beta$, the axial factor is a heat evolution of $\chi$ in the stretched variable), and for every $k\ge0$ the production of either sign inside $|z-a|<Z$ over all time is at most $CG^2\big(1+\log_+(Z/(w_0^2L)^{1/3})\big)$. At the scales of the formal fountain ($Z\sim r_n$, $w_0,L\sim\ell_n$) this is the bound $C(1+\log(1+\alpha_n))$ of (h) for a single cap. It fails for data with many fronts: for $k=1$ the exact solution $\Gamma=G(1-e^{-r^2/2})\big(1+e^{-\sigma(t)}\sin(e^{-2t}z)\big)/2$, $\sigma=(1-e^{-4t})/4$, has, for all sufficiently large $Z$, gross production of each sign at least $cG^2Z$ inside $|z|<Z$ by time $1/10$ (these data have about $Z$ fronts, so they violate the total-variation hypothesis of the one-front bound). In a steady pure-strain cylinder $r<R$ fed through its lateral boundary by one front, $\Gamma(R,z)=\frac g2\big(1+\operatorname{erf}(z\sqrt k)\big)$, the bounded steady solution regular at the axis, with $\Gamma(0,z)=0$, has production inside $|z|<Z$ from the core $r<k^{-1/2}$ of order $g^2k\min(1,\beta)$ with $\beta=Zk^{-1/2}/R^2$, and from the whole cylinder of order $g^2k\,\beta(1+\log(1/\beta))$ when $\beta\le1$ (both for $R\ge2k^{-1/2}$ and $Z\ge k^{-1/2}$, with absolute constants for these data). These calculations illustrate three possible sources of sustained production at the rate $g^2k$ over many inflow times: fronts already present near the axis (about one per core length), a swirl reservoir maintained within radius $(Zk^{-1/2})^{1/2}$ of the axis, or fronts created locally by the last two terms of the identity, that is, by axial variation of the inflow strain. They establish neither necessary nor sufficient conditions for sustained production in a coupled flow. The models do not classify all mechanisms of a coupled flow: by incompressibility the coefficient of $f$ is $-(\partial_zu^z+2u^r/r)=r\partial_r(u^r/r)$, so radial variation of the strain supplies a reaction coefficient that can amplify or damp existing fronts (a positive reaction term alone does not prove net growth against diffusion; the local prescribed incompressible drift $u^r=-kr+cr^3$, $u^z=(2k-4cr^2)z$ has $\partial_zu^r=0$ and coefficient $2cr^2$, positive when $c>0$). These terms are present in irrotational flow (local prescribed-drift illustration: the potential $k_0(z^2-r^2/2)+\varepsilon(z^3-\frac32zr^2)$ has $k'=3\varepsilon$ and $\eta=0$), so the axis value $\eta(0,z)=-k'(z)-\partial_{rr}u^z(0,z)$ does not control them. Localized cell scaling shows that each of the three possibilities is consistent at the formal fountain's scales with the energy, with the swirl part of the dissipation, which equals $2\pi\iint(|\partial_rq|^2+|\partial_zq|^2)r^3\,dr\,dz$ for smooth decaying fields (a cell of size $\ell_n$ and swirl amplitude $G$ dissipates at rate of order $G^2/\ell_n$, so an episode of duration $\ell_n^2$ costs $G^2\ell_n$ and about $\alpha_n$ such episodes cost $G^2r_n\to0$), and with (A) (an axial strain variation of order $\ell_n^{-3}$ is below $H_n=\alpha_n\ell_n^{-3}$, which holds on the formal history because $\alpha_n\nu_n=1$ there). The exact profiles above have infinite energy (their circulation tends to a nonzero constant as $r\to\infty$, and the linear drift is unbounded), and cutoff, reservoir and replenishment costs are not verified, so this is not a finite-energy history. In these models, what the bound of (h) requires is control of the number of axial swirl fronts that reach or are created near the axis along the history, and of the time for which a near-axis swirl reservoir is maintained; no estimate of either is known here. One numerical cap of the type described in (e) was a single burst of this kind: on the axis segment of length four ring radii centred at the cap, $\partial_zq(0,\cdot)$ changed sign once throughout the holding episode and the total variation of $q(0,\cdot)^2$ never exceeded twice its maximum (the single-bump value), so one front was present and none was created. (j) *Persistence of the killed average in simulations* (numerical, exploratory; enters no proof). In axisymmetric simulations at two moderate Reynolds numbers, with the record taken at an off-axis gradient maximum formed by a produced ring (ring radius between $7$ and $32$ core scales, core Reynolds number $M\ell/\nu$ between about $130$ and $440$, $|\omega^\theta|/D$ at the record point between $1.2$ and $1.4$), the killed backward density of the $\eta$ equation was computed from the record point on the annulus of Theorem 7.12 with $\vartheta=1/4$, using the computed velocity history; the exit, source and killed parts of the representation summed to the record value within a few percent. The killed average stayed at the record value until the backward paths left through the inner cylinder, and the paths leaving there carried the full record value, so at this scale the record was fed entirely through the inner boundary, at the age at which the ring had crossed the inner cylinder (its production epoch, or the crossing of material already produced), between about $0.2$ and $0.6$ core diffusion times $\ell^2/\nu$; no persistence beyond that age was observed. At a third, higher Reynolds number (core Reynolds number about $380$, ring radius about $25$ core scales) the gradient maximum of the flow was an axis swirl column, so the point was taken instead at the vorticity maximum of the produced ring ($|\omega^\theta|/D\approx0.84$); the same pattern was observed there, with the drop by inner exit at about $0.23$ core diffusion times, the exiting paths carrying about $80$ to $85$ percent of the record value, and the representation closing only to about $13$ percent because the ring core was resolved by few grid cells. Across the three Reynolds numbers the drop age in core diffusion times was about $0.25$, $0.21$ and $0.23$, flat; three points support no exponent. In these runs the horizon $T_\vartheta$ of Theorem 7.12 is between $10^{-4}$ and $5\cdot10^{-3}$ core diffusion times, because the ratio of the squared ring-to-core ratio to $1+G/\nu$ is at most about $10$; at that short horizon the computed killed term still carried the record value, which is consistent with the persistence inequality of alternative (I), but these finite runs do not verify (I) for a Case 3 sequence, and the asymptotic regime of the theorem, in which this ratio tends to infinity, is not tested. In the final set of runs (two record times at each of the three Reynolds numbers) the same pattern held at the later record times, with drop ages of about $0.57$, $0.35$ and $0.38$ core diffusion times, which measure the age of the material since it crossed the inner cylinder. With the inner cylinder at $\vartheta=1/16$, inside the production zone, a smaller part of the record value leaves through it ($0.13$ to $0.55$), the killed average decays through the conditional average, and the source inside the annulus supplies $0.09$ to $0.63$ of the record value. Following the backward density without inner killing back to the earliest stored times showed where the record's $\eta$ came from (six cases, two record times at each Reynolds number): the source term accounted for $96$ to $97$ percent of the smoothed record value at the two lower Reynolds numbers and about $80$ percent at the third (the same resolution gap), and the pairing with the vorticity at the earliest stored time was below $3$ percent of the record value; the representation closed to about $1$ percent at the two lower Reynolds numbers, and runs with shorter horizons, ending during the production episode, attributed $19$ to $28$ percent of the record value to the source inside the horizon and most of the remainder to vorticity already present at the start of the horizon, consistently with the full-horizon split. The production was concentrated in one early episode (median production time between $2.25$ and $2.75$, against record times of about $10$ and $13$; about $90$ percent of the source contribution was produced before time $4$ to $5$), at median production radius between $0.04$ and $0.13$ ring radii (about one core scale at the two lower Reynolds numbers), that is, at the swirl column near the axis and its immediate surroundings, and it was single-signed along the backward paths (the wrong-sign part was below $1$ percent of the record value). The later records were fed by the same episode as the earlier ones. During that production epoch, up to time about $3.6$, the axial profile of $q^2$ at the first interior radius was a single bump at all three Reynolds numbers (its total variation in $z$ divided by its maximum, which equals $2$ for a single bump, was between $2.00$ and $2.11$), the neighbourhood of the axis of radius $0.1$ in the units of the computation (comparable to the median production radius above) carried $65$ to $81$ percent of the gross production $\int|\partial_z(q^2)|\,dx$ at its peak, and that ratio exceeded $2.3$ only from time about $4.9$ on, after the record's production. So in these computations the record is produced by one axial swirl front in one episode; this says nothing about the number of production renewals that a Case 3 sequence would need. A diagnostic of the gradient integral of Lemma 7.16 (with viscosity $\nu$ the proof of that lemma bounds $\int_{h_1}^{h_2}\int|\nabla p|\,d\mu\,dh$ by $(e\Lambda(h_2-h_1)/\nu)^{1/2}$) gives, on the second half of the persistence interval in five cases, effective values $\nu(\int\!\!\int|\nabla p|\,d\mu\,dh)^2/(e(h_2-h_1))$ between $0.04$ and $0.09$, flat in the Reynolds number, and the axial standard deviation of the killed density stays between about $3$ and $5$ core scales: these records do not use the vertical dispersion measured by the refined logarithm of Lemma 7.38. The effective values and the conversions to core diffusion times were recomputed here from the reported integrals and scales; the runs themselves were not repeated.
